                                     developmentally relevant times?

expression patterns overlap at developmentally relevant times?

17. Conclusion

Phase 2 demonstrates that the chr17:1,411,408–1,841,103 microdeletion is molecularly

         complex.

         The deletion does not merely remove a collection of unrelated genes. It simultaneously

eliminates or severely disrupts multiple regulators of:

   phosphoinositide metabolism;

   phospholipid transport;

   membrane lipid composition;

   endosomal identity;
                                                                                                78

   intracellular trafficking;

   retinal trophic signaling;

   neuronal structural plasticity;

   RNA splicing;

   post-transcriptional RNA regulation;

   protein methylation;

   DNA replication and repair;

   amino-acid transport;

   immune clearance;

   fibrinolysis.

        The strongest mechanistic convergence involves INPP5K, PITPNA, TLCD2, WDR81,

RILP and SERPINF1, with RTN4RL1 extending the system into neuronal surface signaling.

        PRPF8 independently provides definitive evidence for a retinal disease relationship under

established pathogenic variant mechanisms, while RPA1 provides the strongest direct evidence

that heterozygous genomic loss within the interval can produce a measurable human molecular

defect (Clinical Genome Resource, “PRPF8-Related Retinopathy”; O’Driscoll et al.).

        The MIR22HG/MIR22 unit contributes an additional regulatory mechanism with

experimentally established effects on neural response to epileptogenic stress (Silva et al.).

        The central conclusion is therefore not that this deletion has been assigned a phenotype.

        Rather:

The deletion produces a credible multi-gene molecular architecture in which

reduced functional reserve across membrane, endosomal, retinal, RNA-

        regulatory and genome-maintenance systems is biologically plausible.
                                                                                                79

       The magnitude, tissue specificity, developmental timing and clinical consequences of

those reductions remain unresolved.

       Accordingly, Phase 2 establishes mechanistic plausibility and research priorities, not

causality.

       The results provide a sufficiently developed molecular foundation to proceed to

spatiotemporal functional genomics, where the principal hypothesis can be tested without

assuming the clinical conclusion in advance.
                                                                                                80

                                             Phase 3

                              Spatiotemporal Functional Genomics

Phase 3A - Abstract and Methods Summary

Abstract-Ready Phase 3 Summary

Phase 3 was organized into two analytical parts to determine whether the molecular

       mechanisms identified in Phase 2 have the spatial opportunity to operate within the

human visual system. Part I — retinal and ocular localization combined adult whole-tissue

screening with adult human retinal cell-type analysis. Bulk retinal data showed the strongest

expression among affected protein-coding loci for PRPF8, SERPINF1, PITPNA, RPA1,

INPP5K, and TLCD2. Cell-type resolution then identified the clearest spatial convergence in

retinal pigment epithelium, where INPP5K, PITPNA, and SERPINF1 converge on

phosphoinositide regulation, phospholipid handling, and PEDF-associated trophic/lipid

signaling. PRPF8 was broadly represented across neural retina, including photoreceptors, while

TLCD2 showed a preliminary cone-photoreceptor signal. In contrast, the predicted WDR81-

RILP trafficking pair was not demonstrated as a shared adult-retinal neuronal module. Part II —

central visual-relay

specificity tested whether the affected interval preferentially mapped onto magnocellular,

       parvocellular, or koniocellular transcriptional programs in the human dorsal lateral

geniculate nucleus. None of the 17 affected loci appeared among the reproducible human M-

versus-P marker genes or principal K-cell markers reported in the reference dataset. Phase 3

therefore provides evidence about where affected loci are expressed, which visual-system cell
                                                                                                   81

populations can plausibly experience their molecular effects, and whether the deletion

preferentially maps onto a specific visual relay stream. The evidence to date supports a

       distributed retinal and neural functional-reserve model, with the strongest cell-specific

signal centered on RPE membrane/lipid biology, but does not support a selectively

magnocellular lesion or establish a definitive visual-neurological phenotype.

What Phase 3 Provides

Phase 2 established mechanistic plausibility by determining what the affected loci

       normally do. Phase 3 adds spatial opportunity: it tests whether those loci are normally

active in the tissues and cell populations required for the proposed mechanisms to matter in the

human visual system. Through the work completed to date, Phase 3 distinguishes broad retinal

expression from cell-specific localization, tests whether molecularly related deleted genes are

actually co-located in the same retinal compartment, and directly evaluates whether the deletion

preferentially corresponds to one classical thalamic visual pathway. It therefore narrows the

hypothesis without treating expression as proof of dysfunction.

Step-by-Step Method Used Through Phase 3D

Step 1 — Freeze the candidate set and hypotheses before examining expression results

       All 17 structurally affected loci identified in Phase 1 were retained. No gene was

removed because Phase 2 had assigned it weak visual or neurological relevance. The primary

Phase 3 hypotheses were frozen in advance, including membrane/endosomal co-

localization,

       direct retinal localization, WDR81-RILP overlap, developmental enrichment,

magnocellular specificity, and regulatory overlap. This was done to reduce phenotype-driven

selection and confirmation bias.
                                                                                                   82

Step 2 — Separate loci according to assay compatibility

       Protein-coding genes were treated as generally measurable by conventional bulk and

single-cell RNA sequencing. PITPNA-AS1 and MIR22HG were retained with the recognition

that lncRNAs can be poorly captured in some single-cell platforms. Mature MIR22 was not

interpreted from ordinary poly(A)-selected RNA-seq because mature microRNAs require small-

RNA or miRNA-specific measurement. RN7SL105P was treated cautiously because pseudogene

homology can cause ambiguous short-read mapping.

Step 3 — Screen adult whole-body tissue expression

       Adult expression resources including GTEx and NCBI Gene summaries were used to

determine whether each affected protein-coding locus was broadly expressed, brain biased,

peripheral-tissue enriched, or strongly tissue specific. This established general biological context

before any retina-specific ranking was performed. Cross-platform expression magnitudes were

not treated as directly interchangeable.

Step 4 — Perform the initial adult human retinal screen

       Human ocular resources, especially eyeIntegration and the Human Protein Atlas retinal

series, were used to determine which affected genes were detectably expressed in adult retina.

The strongest bulk retinal signals among the affected protein-coding genes were PRPF8,

SERPINF1, PITPNA, RPA1, INPP5K, and TLCD2. Genes showing weak whole-retina

abundance were not discarded because bulk measurements can dilute expression from rare cell

populations.

Step 5 — Resolve the retinal signal to individual cell classes

       Large human retinal single-cell atlases and independent adult human retinal datasets were

used to test localization among photoreceptors, bipolar cells, amacrine cells, horizontal cells,
                                                                                              83

retinal ganglion cells, Müller glia, astrocytes, microglia, vascular populations, and RPE.

Published marker frameworks were used as quality-control references for identifying major

retinal populations.

Step 6 — Test whether the Phase 2 molecular convergence survives cell-type resolution

       The pre-specified membrane/endosomal candidate set was examined for shared

localization. The strongest supported adult-retinal convergence became an RPE-centered

INPP5K-PITPNA-SERPINF1 module. PRPF8 was broadly present across neural retina,

and

       TLCD2 showed a preliminary cone-associated signal. Conversely, WDR81 and RILP

were not demonstrated as a shared mature retinal neuronal module. Negative results were

retained rather than redefining the candidate set after inspection.

Step 7 — Move from retina to the central visual relay

       Human dorsal lateral geniculate nucleus single-nucleus RNA-seq data from the

Allen/Bakken comparative LGN resource were used to test the pre-specified magnocellular

hypothesis. The analysis treated magnocellular, parvocellular, and koniocellular identities

symmetrically rather than defining magnocellular enrichment as the desired result.

Step 8 — Compare affected loci with published human M/P/K transcriptional markers

       The human dLGN study showed that M and P projection neurons largely form one

transcriptomic MP population with a graded M-to-P continuum. The published reproducible M/P

marker set and principal K-cell markers were compared against all 17 affected loci. None of the

affected loci was a defining M, P, or K marker. The result was therefore recorded as a

meaningful negative finding: current adult human dLGN transcriptomics does not support

preferential magnocellular targeting by the deletion.
                                                                                                  84

Step 9 — Update the model without converting expression into causality

       The evidence from the retinal and dLGN analyses was integrated with the Phase 2

mechanisms. Expression was interpreted only as opportunity for a mechanism, not proof that the

deletion produces dysfunction. The working visual-system model therefore shifted away from a

selectively magnocellular lesion toward a distributed retinal and neural functional-

reserve model, with the strongest present spatial convergence in RPE. No definitive

phenotype

       was assigned.

Current Phase 3 Interpretation

       Through Phase 3D, the study has established that several molecularly important deleted

genes are normally positioned within biologically relevant human visual-system compartments.

The strongest cell-type-specific convergence is INPP5K + PITPNA + SERPINF1 in RPE, while

PRPF8 has broad neural-retinal availability and TLCD2 may contribute a cone-associated lipid

component. At the same time, the data weaken two earlier possibilities: WDR81 and RILP have

not been demonstrated as a shared adult-retinal trafficking module, and none of the affected loci

demonstrates published magnocellular-specific transcriptional enrichment in adult human dLGN.

The Phase 3 evidence therefore supports continued investigation of a distributed retinal/neural

mechanism while rejecting, at the present level of evidence, a specifically magnocellular

transcriptomic explanation.

Phase 3 remains in progress. Human V1 and developmental brain/retinal analyses have

       not yet been completed and must not be represented as results.
                                                                                                  85

Phase 3B - Research Through Phase 3D

Document scope. This document reproduces the Phase 3 research conducted through

Phase 3D. The wording of the research sections is preserved; prior hyperlink-style source

notes

        have been converted to MLA-style parenthetical citations. Phase 3B was defined in the

frozen protocol but was not executed as a separate results section before Phase 3C, so its

protocol language is included with an explicit scope note rather than with invented results.

Phase 3A — Adult Whole-Tissue and Initial Ocular Expression Screen

1. Objective

Phase 3A asks whether the 17 loci affected by the 17p13.3 microdeletion are

        transcriptionally active in adult human tissues relevant to the project's visual-neurological

hypothesis.

        The purpose is not to infer disease from expression.

        Instead, Phase 3A establishes the first requirement for biological plausibility:

A deleted locus must normally be expressed in the relevant tissue before

reduced dosage can plausibly perturb that tissue through its canonical

        molecular function.

        All 17 loci remain under consideration.

2. Data Sources

        The current GTEx adult bulk-expression release is GTEx Analysis V11. The portal

provides gene-level TPM and median tissue-expression matrices, including the current V11

median gene-level TPM file. ("GTEx Portal")
                                                                                                  86

       For ocular-specific analysis, eyeIntegration v2.12 integrates publicly available healthy

human ocular RNA-seq datasets with a substantial subset of GTEx samples processed through a

common pipeline. It provides downloadable gene- and transcript-level raw-count matrices and

includes multiple ocular tissue categories. ("eyeIntegration")

       The initial retinal screen below additionally uses current Human Protein Atlas retinal

RNA-expression pages. These provide a 105-sample adult human retinal series and consensus

normalized transcript measurements. These values are useful for screening but will not substitute

for the independent eyeIntegration and single-cell replication planned later in Phase 3.

       Current NCBI Gene expression summaries were used as an independent whole-body

localization check.

3. Whole-Body Expression Pattern

       The first result is that the deleted interval does not consist predominantly of retinaspecific

or brain-specific genes.

       Most affected protein-coding genes are broadly or ubiquitously expressed.

       This is methodologically important.

       A visual-neurological mechanism, if present, will therefore probably depend on:

cell type + developmental context + dosage sensitivity

       rather than simply: brain-specific gene expression.

       The current Human Protein Atlas retinal series provides an initial quantitative screen for

the 13 protein-coding genes.

6. First Major Phase 3 Result: PRPF8 Is Strongly Represented in Adult Retina

        PRPF8 is the highest-expressed affected protein-coding gene in the initial retinal screen

at approximately:

76.4 nTPM.

        ("PRPF8 Retina")

        This substantially strengthens the Phase 2 observation that PRPF8 is biologically relevant

to retinal tissue.

        However, the significance is specific:
                                                                                                93

PRPF8 has both strong retinal expression and independently established

         retinal disease biology.

         It still does not establish that whole-gene heterozygous deletion produces the same

mechanism as known pathogenic PRPF8 variants.

7. SERPINF1 Is Also Strongly Expressed in Retina

         SERPINF1 is expressed at approximately:

56.9 nTPM

         in the adult retinal series. ("SERPINF1 Retina")

         This is particularly meaningful because Phase 2 independently established PEDF as a:

   retinal neurotrophic factor;

   antiangiogenic factor;

   regulator of retinal survival;

   participant in PEDF-R-linked phospholipid signaling.

         The Phase 3A result therefore provides direct spatial opportunity for the Phase 2

mechanism.

         SERPINF1 is not merely a systemic secreted gene with incidental retinal literature.

         It is substantially expressed within adult retinal tissue.

8. PITPNA Provides a Strong Link Between the Phase 2 Lipid Cluster and Retina

         PITPNA is expressed at approximately:

43.9 nTPM

         in adult retina. ("PITPNA Retina")

         NCBI also identifies brain as one of PITPNA's highest-expression tissues. ("PITPNA

Gene")
                                                                                                   94

       This makes PITPNA one of the first genes for which Phase 3 supports both:

adult neural expression

       and: substantial retinal expression.

       Because PITPNA was already one of the strongest Phase 2 phosphoinositide-homeostasis

candidates, this finding materially strengthens the membrane-lipid hypothesis.

9. INPP5K Is Clearly Present in Adult Retina

       INPP5K averages approximately:

14.7 nTPM

       in the retinal series. ("INPP5K Retina")

       More importantly, current Human Protein Atlas single-cell annotations classify INPP5K

as group enriched in retinal pigment epithelial cells and late spermatids, providing an early

indication that its retinal bulk expression may contain a strong RPE component. ("INPP5K

Single Cell")

       This observation is significant because it begins to localize one of the central

phosphoinositide enzymes from Phase 2 to a specific ocular cell population.

       It will require confirmation in the dedicated retinal single-cell phase.

10. RPA1 Is Substantially Expressed in Adult Retina

       RPA1 shows approximately:

20.8 nTPM

       with relatively limited variation across the 105 retinal samples. ("RPA1 Retina")

       This fits its ubiquitous genome-maintenance role.

       The result establishes that the tissue contains substantial RPA1 transcript and therefore

that the Phase 2 haploinsufficiency mechanism is molecularly available in retinal cells.
                                                                                                95

       It does not establish that retina is preferentially vulnerable to RPA1 loss.

11. RTN4RL1 Is Detectable in Retina and Brain

       RTN4RL1 shows approximately:

7.9 nTPM

       in adult retina. ("RTN4RL1 Retina")

       Its whole-body pattern is additionally brain biased, with NCBI listing brain as its highest

reported human tissue. ("RTN4RL1 Gene")

       This supports the Phase 2 interpretation of RTN4RL1 as a legitimate

neural/visualpathway candidate rather than a receptor whose optic-nerve biology is disconnected

from human tissue expression.

       The next decisive question is:

Which retinal population expresses RTN4RL1?

       If it localizes primarily to retinal ganglion cells, that would align closely with the

opticnerve regeneration mechanism described in Phase 2.

       If it instead localizes to another population, the working model must be revised

accordingly.

12. TLCD2 Is Detectable at Moderate Levels

       TLCD2 shows approximately:

10.1 nTPM

       in adult retina. ("TLCD2 Retina")

       This is an important result because TLCD2 had weak direct retinal evidence in Phase 2.
                                                                                                 96

Phase 3A therefore upgrades one specific proposition:

The TLCD2 phospholipid-composition mechanism has an opportunity to

       operate in adult retinal tissue.

       It does not yet establish which retinal cells express TLCD2 or whether one-copy

reduction meaningfully alters retinal membrane composition.

13. WDR81 and RILP Produce an Important Bulk-Tissue Warning

       WDR81 and RILP were among the strongest neural trafficking candidates in Phase 2.

       Yet whole-retina expression is low:

WDR81 ≈ 0.7 nTPM

       RILP ≈ 0.4 nTPM.

       ("WDR81 Retina"; "RILP Retina")

       This does not currently falsify the WDR81–RILP hypothesis.

       Instead, there are at least three possibilities:

1. They are genuinely weakly expressed in adult retina.

       That would weaken a retinal mechanism.

2. They are expressed in a small retinal population.

       Bulk retinal RNA would dilute such a signal.

3. Their principal relevance lies outside retina, for example in dLGN, cortex,

developmental

       progenitors, or other neural tissues.

Phase 3C–G will discriminate among these possibilities.

       This is exactly why bulk tissue analysis was performed before cell-type interpretation.
                                                                                                    97

14. SCARF1 and SERPINF2 Behave as Predicted from Phase 2

       SCARF1 and SERPINF2 show only approximately:

0.2 nTPM

       and:

0.5 nTPM

       respectively in adult retina. ("SCARF1 Retina"; "SERPINF2 Retina")

       Their whole-body patterns are much more compatible with:

SCARF1 → immune/endothelial biology

       and: SERPINF2 → hepatic/systemic fibrinolysis.

       This is a useful negative result.

Phase 3A currently provides no reason to elevate either gene as a direct retinal candidate.

       They remain in subsequent unbiased analyses but should not receive retinal priority

merely because they fall inside the deletion.

15. SLC43A2 Remains Weak as a Direct Retinal Candidate

       SLC43A2 shows only approximately:

1.3 nTPM

       in adult retina. ("SLC43A2 Retina")

       Combined with its placenta/kidney-biased whole-body pattern, this further supports its

Phase 2 classification as primarily an epithelial nutrient-transport gene rather than a

leading

       direct visual-system candidate. ("SLC43A2 Gene")

       A specialized retinal cell population could still express it, so it remains in the single-cell

analysis.
                                                                                                   98

16. SMYD4 Is Detectable but Not Retinally Exceptional

         SMYD4 averages approximately:

7.2 nTPM

         in adult retina. ("SMYD4 Retina")

         Its whole-body expression is otherwise ubiquitous and relatively modest. ("SMYD4

Gene")

         Thus Phase 3A establishes that SMYD4 is available to operate in retinal tissue but does

not provide evidence of retinal enrichment.

         This is consistent with its Phase 2 classification as a broad regulatory gene rather than a

direct retinal candidate.

17. Initial Assessment of the Phase 2 Membrane/Endosomal Cluster

         The pre-specified Phase 2 cluster was:

INPP5K + PITPNA + TLCD2 + WDR81 + RILP + SERPINF1

         The adult retinal bulk screen gives:

       The first four genes therefore satisfy the most basic spatial criterion:

       retinal tissue.

       The two endosomal-trafficking genes do not yet satisfy that criterion strongly at the

whole-retina level.

       Consequently, the Phase 2 convergence is partially supported rather than fully supported

by Phase 3A.

       That distinction should be preserved.

18. Unexpectedly Strong Early Result: INPP5K–PITPNA–TLCD2–SERPINF1

       Before single-cell analysis, four components of the membrane/lipid model already show

measurable adult retinal expression:
                                                                                                 100

PITPNA

       PI/PC transfer

       +

INPP5K

       phosphoinositide turnover

       +

TLCD2

       phospholipid acyl-chain regulation

       +

SERPINF1

       PEDF-linked retinal lipid signaling.

       This provides a preliminary spatial bridge across four molecular levels:

lipid availability → phosphoinositide chemistry → membrane composition →

       extracellular retinal lipid signaling.

       The result is stronger than Phase 2 alone because these genes are not simply related in

abstract molecular databases.

       Their transcripts are all present within the same gross tissue compartment:

       adult human retina.

       However, whole-retina co-presence does not prove they occur in the same cells.

       That becomes the central Phase 3C test.

19. PRPF8 Creates an Independent High-Expression Retinal Axis

       PRPF8's adult retinal expression is substantially higher than the membrane candidates

except SERPINF1/PITPNA.
                                                                                               101

        This means the deletion contains, within the same retinal tissue:

highly expressed RNA-splicing machinery

        and: multiple expressed membrane/lipid regulators.

        The later coexpression question is therefore particularly important:

Does PRPF8 occupy the same retinal cell classes that express the membrane-

homeostasis genes?

        If so, there would be spatial opportunity for splicing perturbation and membrane

perturbation to coexist within the same cells.

        If not, the mechanisms may remain biologically separated.

20. Noncoding Loci

        The noncoding loci require separate treatment.

PITPNA-AS1 and MIR22HG

        Standard bulk RNA-seq can potentially detect these lncRNAs, but lower abundance,

transcript annotation, and library-selection differences make cross-platform absence difficult to

interpret.

        They will therefore be analyzed through:

   eyeIntegration gene/transcript matrices;

   appropriate retinal single-cell datasets where captured;

   developmental datasets.

MIR22

        Mature miR-22 is not validly quantified from conventional poly(A) mRNA RNA-seq.

        MIR22 will therefore be excluded from the ordinary mRNA-expression ranking and

analyzed later using small-RNA/miRNA-specific datasets.
                                                                                              102

RN7SL105P

       Because RN7SL105P is a highly homologous pseudogene, apparent RNA-seq reads may

be ambiguously mapped.

       It will not receive an expression score unless locus-specific mapping quality can be

established.
                                                                                                 103

22. Hypothesis Status After Phase 3A

H1 — Membrane/endosomal co-localization

       Partially supported.

       PITPNA, INPP5K, TLCD2, and SERPINF1 are clearly present in adult retina.

       WDR81 and RILP are weak in whole-retina bulk and require cell-type testing.

H2 — Direct retinal localization

       Partially supported at bulk level.

       PRPF8, SERPINF1, and RTN4RL1 are all detectable in adult retina, with PRPF8 and

SERPINF1 particularly strong.

H3 — WDR81/RILP neural-trafficking overlap

       Unresolved.

       Whole-retina abundance is low for both.

H4 — Developmental enrichment

       Not yet tested.

H5 — Magnocellular specificity

       Not yet tested.

H6 — Regulatory overlap

       Not yet tested at cell-type resolution.

23. Main Interpretation

       The first in silico expression layer supports the project enough to continue, but it does not

simply confirm the Phase 2 model.

       Instead, it modifies it.

       The strongest retinal molecular set at this stage is:
                                                                                                  105

PRPF8 + SERPINF1 + PITPNA + RPA1 + INPP5K + TLCD2

       with RTN4RL1 also clearly detectable.

       The specific Phase 2 membrane convergence is narrowed provisionally to:

PITPNA + INPP5K + TLCD2 + SERPINF1

       because those four demonstrate substantial adult retinal expression.

       WDR81 and RILP remain highly credible neuronal proteins but are not strongly

supported as whole-retina adult mechanisms from this first dataset.

       That is a meaningful negative/qualifying result rather than a failure of the hypothesis.

24. Phase 3A Working Conclusion

Phase 3A demonstrates that several of the strongest Phase 2 candidate mechanisms

       possess clear spatial opportunity within adult human retina.

       Most notably:

PITPNA

INPP5K

TLCD2

       and:

SERPINF1

       are simultaneously represented in adult retinal tissue, supporting the existence of an

ocular membrane/lipid-regulatory subnetwork.

       PRPF8 is strongly expressed in the same gross tissue, while RPA1 is also substantially

expressed and RTN4RL1 is clearly detectable.
                                                                                                 106

         Conversely, WDR81, RILP, SCARF1, SERPINF2, and SLC43A2 show low whole-retina

expression, emphasizing that molecular importance identified in Phase 2 does not automatically

imply adult retinal importance.

         The appropriate conclusion is therefore:

Phase 3A provides preliminary spatial support for a subset of the Phase 2

visual-neural model, especially the PITPNA–INPP5K–TLCD2–SERPINF1

membrane/lipid axis, while leaving the WDR81–RILP trafficking component

unresolved and weakening direct retinal prioritization of several systemic

         genes.

         The next analysis must move below the tissue level.

         Whole-retina RNA cannot determine whether these genes occur in the same biological

cells.

         The decisive next question is:

Which retinal cell populations—rods, cones, bipolar cells, amacrine cells,

retinal ganglion cells, Müller glia, RPE, vascular cells, or immune cells—

express each affected locus?

Phase 3B — Human Ocular Bulk Expression

Editorial scope note: Phase 3B was defined in the frozen Phase 3 protocol but was not

         executed as a separate results section before Phase 3C. Its initial ocular bulk screen was

incorporated into Phase 3A above. The original Phase 3B protocol text is reproduced verbatim

here so that the Phase 3 record remains complete without inventing a standalone result.

         GTEx alone is insufficient for detailed eye biology.
                                                                                              107

         The primary ocular bulk resource will be eyeIntegration, which integrates healthy human

ocular RNA-seq datasets processed through a consistent pipeline and provides gene- and

transcript-level expression matrices. ("eyeIntegration")

         The ocular analysis should include, where available:

     neural retina;

     retinal pigment epithelium;

     choroid;

     optic nerve;

     cornea;

     other ocular tissues as internal comparisons.

         The main objective is to determine whether the Phase 2 candidates are actually enriched

in:

         neural retina and/or RPE relative to non-neural ocular tissues.

Phase 3C — Adult Human Retinal Single-Cell Localization

1. Objective

Phase 3A established that several affected genes are detectably expressed in adult human

         retina at the whole-tissue level.

Phase 3C asks the more discriminating question:

Which adult human retinal cell populations actually express the affected loci,

and do the Phase 2 mechanistically related genes occupy the same cellular

compartments?

         This distinction is essential because bulk retinal RNA-seq can generate apparent

convergence even when different genes are expressed in completely different cell populations.
                                                                                                  108

        The principal cell classes considered are:

rod photoreceptors, cone photoreceptors, bipolar cells, amacrine cells, horizontal

cells, retinal ganglion cells, Müller glia, astrocytes, microglia, and retinal pigment epithelial

        cells.

        RPE is treated separately from the neural retina because it is an epithelial support layer

rather than a retinal neuron, despite its critical role in photoreceptor physiology.

2. Reference Data

        The principal contemporary reference is the 2026 Human Retina Cell Atlas, which

integrated approximately 3.9 million cells from 125 human donors and identified more than

130 retinal cell types. The atlas integrates multiple published studies with approximately

2.7

        million previously unpublished observations and includes both transcriptomic and

chromatinaccessibility information. (Li et al.)

        For replication and interpretation, older independent human retinal datasets remain

useful. The 2019 Lukowski atlas profiled 20,009 cells from three healthy adult donors and

resolved all major neural retinal populations, including rods, cones, bipolar cells, amacrine cells,

retinal ganglion cells, horizontal cells, Müller glia, astrocytes, and microglia. (Lukowski et al.)

        The current gene-level screen uses accessible Human Protein Atlas single-cell

annotations together with independent retinal studies. Because some HPA classifications vary

between releases and because the largest 2026 atlas is distributed primarily through interactive

and downloadable single-cell resources, release-dependent signals are classified as preliminary

rather than treated as replicated facts.
                                                                                               109

3. Evidence Classification

Phase 3C uses four expression-evidence levels.

Level

Interpretation

        Reproduced across independent human data or supported

3 — Strong

        by current single-cell evidence plus orthogonal retinal

        evidence

        Convincing localization in one major human single-cell

2 — Moderate

        resource

        Suggestive, release-dependent, low-abundance, or

1 — Preliminary

        indirectly localized

0 — Unsupported

        No reliable adult retinal cell-type localization identified

        An unsupported result does not mean that the gene is biologically absent. Low

expression, technical dropout, library chemistry, or rare-cell localization may prevent detection.

5. INPP5K Localizes Strongly Toward Retinal Pigment Epithelium

       Current Human Protein Atlas single-cell annotation classifies INPP5K as:

group enriched in retinal pigment epithelial cells and late spermatids

       and places it in the expression cluster:

Retinal pigment epithelial cells — Retinol metabolism. ("INPP5K Single

       Cell")

       This substantially improves the Phase 3A interpretation.

       INPP5K is not merely present somewhere in whole retinal tissue. Its expression appears

preferentially associated with the RPE compartment.

       That provides cellular opportunity for its Phase 2 phosphoinositide mechanism to operate

at the interface responsible for:

photoreceptor support

       ↓

outer-segment turnover

       ↓
                                                                                              115

retinoid cycling

       ↓ membrane trafficking and metabolic exchange.

       The current evidence does not show that INPP5K loss disrupts these functions. It

establishes only that the enzyme is preferentially positioned within an ocular compartment where

intensive membrane processing occurs.

Phase 3C status

       RPE localization: supported. Photoreceptor localization: not established as the principal

signal. RGC localization: not established.

6. PITPNA Also Shows RPE Enhancement

       PITPNA independently shows cell-type-enhanced expression in retinal pigment

epithelial cells, in addition to several nonocular populations. ("PITPNA Single Cell")

       This result is important because PITPNA and INPP5K were already independently linked

in Phase 2 through phosphatidylinositol biology.

       Their molecular functions differ:

PITPNA

                → phosphatidylinositol/phosphatidylcholine binding and transfer

       whereas:

INPP5K

                          → phosphoinositide 5-phosphate hydrolysis.

Phase 3C now adds a spatial relationship:

       both are preferentially represented in RPE-associated single-cell expression.

       This is substantially stronger evidence of functional convergence than their mere

presence in the same whole-retina sample.
                                                                                            116

       It still does not demonstrate physical interaction or epistasis.

Phase 3C status

       INPP5K + PITPNA RPE co-localization: supported at the cell-class level.

       The appropriate term is:

       RPE-centered functional convergence.

7. SERPINF1 Produces the Strongest RPE Signal

       SERPINF1/PEDF is currently classified as group enriched in retinal pigment epithelial

cells and decidual stromal cells in the Human Protein Atlas. ("SERPINF1 Single Cell")

       Independent human retinal single-cell work also identified an RPE cluster characterized

by high SERPINF1 and RLBP1 expression. (Voigt et al.)

       This provides replication across independent human evidence.

       SERPINF1 therefore receives: Expression Evidence Level 3 — Strong.

       The result aligns closely with its established PEDF biology.

Phase 2 showed that PEDF contributes to:

   retinal trophic support;

   antiangiogenic regulation;

   photoreceptor survival;

   extracellular-matrix signaling;

   PEDF-R/PNPLA2-linked lipid metabolism.

Phase 3C now localizes this mechanism strongly to:

       retinal pigment epithelium.

8. An RPE-Centered Molecular Module Now Emerges

       The strongest new Phase 3C result is therefore:

       Importantly, it differs from the original broad Phase 2 model.

       The strongest membrane/lipid convergence in adult retina presently appears to occur

9. Why the RPE Finding Matters

       RPE cells perform unusually membrane-intensive functions.

       They support photoreceptors through:

   outer-segment phagocytosis;

   retinoid recycling;

   nutrient transport;

   ion and water regulation;

   growth-factor secretion;

   lipid handling.

       Therefore a convergence of lipid and phosphoinositide regulators within RPE is

biologically coherent.

       However, this observation must not be converted prematurely into:

       RPE dysfunction causes the candidate visual phenotype.

       At this stage the correct conclusion is narrower:

The deletion simultaneously affects several genes whose normal expression

       and molecular functions converge within RPE cells.

       That establishes a plausible cellular site of combined dosage perturbation.
                                                                                                119

10. PRPF8 Is Broadly Expressed Across Neural Retina

       Earlier HPA releases classified PRPF8 as enhanced in rod photoreceptor cells and placed

it within a photoreceptor/visual-perception expression cluster. ("PRPF8 Single Cell")

       However, more recent evidence argues against interpreting PRPF8 as primarily

rodspecific.

       A 2026 human retinal-organoid study reported PRPF8 expression throughout the neural

retina, including both rod and cone photoreceptors, and noted that this distribution is consistent

with existing human retinal single-cell data showing broadly uniform retinal expression.

(Zimmann et al.)

       Therefore the stronger interpretation is:

PRPF8 is broadly required across retinal cell populations, with robust

       photoreceptor expression, rather than being a uniquely rod-restricted gene.

       This fits its molecular role as a core spliceosomal protein.

Phase 3C status

       Adult neural-retinal expression: strong. Rod expression: strong. Cone expression: strong.

Strict rod specificity: not supported.

11. PRPF8 Therefore Represents a Different Type of Retinal Risk

       The RPE module involves cell-enriched membrane and trophic functions.

       PRPF8 is different.

       Because it is a core RNA-processing protein expressed broadly through neural retina,

reduced PRPF8 dosage—if functionally important—could potentially affect multiple retinal cell

populations simultaneously.

       Thus:
                                                                                              120

RPE module

                        → spatially concentrated biochemical convergence

         whereas:

PRPF8

                       → broad retinal information-processing vulnerability.

         The two mechanisms could coexist in the same tissue without being part of the same

direct pathway.

12. TLCD2 Shows a Possible Cone-Photoreceptor Signal

         The Human Protein Atlas v24 classification identifies TLCD2 as cell-type enhanced in

cone photoreceptor cells, along with several nonocular cell populations. ("TLCD2 Single

Cell")

         This is potentially important because TLCD2 regulates phospholipid acyl-chain

composition and membrane fluidity.

         Cone photoreceptors depend on specialized membrane architecture and intense lipid

turnover.

         However, the TLCD2 signal is currently less secure than the RPE findings because

celltype classifications have varied across HPA releases.

         Therefore TLCD2 receives:

         Expression Evidence Level 1–2: preliminary to moderate.

         The current hypothesis is:

TLCD2 dosage loss

         ↓

         possible altered phospholipid composition within cone-associated cells
                                                                                                 121

       but not: TLCD2 loss causes cone dysfunction.

       Replication in the full 2026 Human Retina Cell Atlas should be required before assigning

strong cell-type specificity.

13. The Lipid Cluster Therefore Splits Into at Least Two Adult Retinal Compartments

Phase 3A suggested a broad retinal set:

       PITPNA + INPP5K + TLCD2 + SERPINF1.

Phase 3C now indicates that this set is not homogeneous.

       The strongest current organization is:

RPE-associated module

PITPNA

INPP5K

SERPINF1

Possible photoreceptor-associated component

TLCD2

       particularly:

cone photoreceptors

       This distinction is scientifically important.

       The deletion may perturb related aspects of membrane biology in adjacent but different

retinal compartments rather than through a single intracellular pathway operating in one cell.

14. WDR81 Does Not Show Strong Adult Retinal Neuronal Enrichment

       WDR81 is classified by the Human Protein Atlas as cell-type enhanced in microglia,

together with several non-neural cell types. ("WDR81 Single Cell")
                                                                                              122

Phase 3A also found only approximately 0.7 nTPM in whole-retina bulk tissue.

       Thus the current adult-retinal evidence does not support strong WDR81 expression in:

   rods;

   cones;

   bipolar neurons;

   amacrine neurons;

   retinal ganglion cells.

       The available signal instead suggests a possible microglial association.

       This does not weaken WDR81's established neurodevelopmental importance.

       It changes where that importance should be investigated.

       WDR81 may prove more relevant to:

   developmental neural progenitors;

   brain neurons;

   glial populations;

   dLGN;

   cortical systems

       than to mature retinal neurons.

Phase 3C status

       Adult retinal-neuronal hypothesis: weakened. Glial/microglial possibility: retained.

Developmental neural hypothesis: still open.

15. RILP Also Fails to Show a Strong Retina-Specific Cell-Type Signal

       RILP is associated broadly with a neuronal neurotransmission/signaling expression
                                                                                               123

cluster, and current HPA data classify it as enhanced in “other brain neurons” among

several

          non-neural populations. ("RILP Single Cell")

          However, no convincing adult-retinal cell-type enrichment emerged from the current

screen.

          Combined with its very low whole-retina bulk abundance, this argues against making

RILP a leading adult retinal mechanism at present.

          This is an important negative result.

Phase 2 demonstrated strong neuronal RILP biology.

Phase 3C now suggests that the best place to test that biology may be:

          central visual-system neurons rather than mature retina.

          Therefore the forthcoming dLGN and V1 analyses become especially important for

RILP.

16. The Original WDR81–RILP Retinal Convergence Is Not Supported

Phase 2 predicted that WDR81 and RILP might form a coherent endosomal sequence:

WDR81

                                       → endosome maturation

          ↓

RAB7-positive late endosome

          ↓

RILP

                                        → organelle transport.

          At the molecular level this remains plausible.
                                                                                               124

        At the adult-retina expression level, however, Phase 3C does not currently demonstrate

that both genes are strongly expressed in the same retinal neuronal population.

        WDR81 shows a microglia-associated signal.

        RILP shows a broader neuronal signal without clear retina-specific enrichment.

        Therefore:

        H3 is not supported in adult retina at this stage.

        This does not falsify the molecular pathway.

        It means the pathway's spatial opportunity has not yet been demonstrated in mature

retinal cells.

        It should now be tested in the dLGN, V1, and developmental datasets.

17. RTN4RL1: An Important Negative/Unresolved Finding

Phase 2 identified RTN4RL1 as a strong neural candidate because Nogo-receptor-family

        biology directly affects retinal ganglion-cell axons and optic-nerve regeneration.

Phase 3A showed detectable whole-retina expression.

        However, current HPA single-cell classification does not identify retinal ganglion cells as

an RTN4RL1-enriched population. Instead, the strongest whole-body single-cell specificity

appears in spermatogenic cells, while human brain single-nucleus expression shows low overall

cell-type specificity. ("RTN4RL1 Single Cell")

        Human Protein Atlas protein-level data do support broader neural expression, including

neurons, astrocytic/neuropil and microglial/neuropil compartments, but that does not establish a

retinal ganglion-cell-specific signal. ("RTN4RL1 HPA")

        Therefore the hypothesis:
                                                                                                   125

RTN4RL1 is specifically enriched in adult human retinal ganglion cells

          is not currently supported.

          This is important because it prevents the Phase 2 optic-nerve literature from being

converted into an expression claim that the data do not yet support.

Phase 3C status

          Retinal presence: supported by bulk RNA. RGC-specific enrichment: unresolved/not

demonstrated. Central neural relevance: still strongly plausible.

18. RPA1 Appears to Behave as a Broad Cellular Maintenance Gene

          RPA1 has low overall cell-type specificity in current single-cell resources, with

enrichment signals occurring outside the retina, particularly in proliferative or specialized

nonretinal populations. Its broader expression pattern is classified as consistent with basic

cellular processes. ("RPA1 Single Cell")

          This is consistent with RPA1's molecular function.

          RPA1 is required for fundamental:

   ssDNA protection;

   replication;

   repair;

   ATR signaling.

          Its substantial retinal bulk expression therefore likely reflects a widely distributed

cellular maintenance requirement, rather than specialization within a particular mature

retinal

          neuron.

          This matters for interpretation.
                                                                                               126

           RPA1 remains the strongest direct heterozygous molecular dosage candidate from Phase

2, but Phase 3C provides no evidence that its dosage vulnerability is retina-specific.

19. SMYD4 Also Shows No Specialized Adult Retinal Localization

           SMYD4 currently demonstrates low cell-type specificity overall and no convincing

retinal-specific enrichment. Its strongest HPA single-cell enhancement is reported in myonuclei

rather than ocular neurons. ("SMYD4 Single Cell")

           Thus its Phase 3A retinal bulk expression should be interpreted as:

general expression compatible with a broad regulatory role

           rather than:

           evidence for a specialized retinal epigenetic mechanism.

           The best later opportunity for SMYD4 may be developmental analysis, where epigenetic

regulators can have transient effects not apparent in mature tissue.

20. SLC43A2 Shows a Possible Microglial Association

           SLC43A2 is not strongly expressed in whole adult retina.

           Current single-cell resources nevertheless show an elevated signal involving

microglia/neuropil among several other cell populations, while its overall strongest

expression

           remains associated with renal and epithelial transport systems. ("SLC43A2 Single Cell")

           This supports a limited possibility that SLC43A2 has an immune/glial role within neural

tissues.

           However, the evidence does not support elevating SLC43A2 into the principal

neuralretinal pathway.
                                                                                                   127

Phase 3C status

       Photoreceptor role: unsupported. Retinal neuronal role: unsupported. Possible microglial

contribution: preliminary.

21. SCARF1 Is Consistent With a Glial/Vascular Rather Than Neuronal Role

       SCARF1 shows strong cell-type specificity for several non-neuronal populations,

including microglia and astrocytes, and its expression cluster is annotated as brain endothelial

cells — blood-brain barrier. ("SCARF1 Single Cell")

       This is consistent with its Phase 2 role in:

   scavenger recognition;

   apoptotic-cell clearance;

   immune homeostasis.

       Its very low adult-retinal bulk expression makes it unlikely to represent a major

photoreceptor or retinal-neuron mechanism.

       Instead, if it proves relevant to the visual system, the more plausible route would involve:

       immune, glial, endothelial, or clearance biology.

22. SERPINF2 Remains Essentially Non-Retinal

       SERPINF2 shows strong single-cell enrichment in proximal tubular cells, with

liverassociated systemic expression and only very low adult-retinal bulk abundance.

("SERPINF2 Single Cell")

       No compelling mature retinal neuronal or RPE cell-type signal emerged.

       This strongly reinforces the Phase 2 classification:

SERPINF2 is primarily a systemic hemostatic locus rather than a direct

       retinal gene.
                                                                                                128

        Its inclusion in later retinal analyses should remain as an unbiased negative control rather

than because there is currently a positive retinal hypothesis.

23. Noncoding Loci Require a Separate Assay Strategy

        The four noncoding loci cannot be treated like ordinary protein-coding genes.

PITPNA-AS1

        As a lncRNA, PITPNA-AS1 may have low abundance and can be poorly captured by

droplet-based or 3′-biased scRNA-seq.

        Current absence of a strong cell-type annotation is therefore:

        unresolved, not evidence of true biological absence.

MIR22HG

        The same limitation applies to MIR22HG.

        Its host-transcript abundance may be measurable in appropriate total-RNA datasets, but

conventional retinal single-cell assays may underrepresent it.

MIR22

        Mature miR-22 cannot be validly inferred from ordinary poly(A)-selected scRNA-seq.

        Its Phase 3 localization requires: small-RNA sequencing or miRNA-specific expression

data.

RN7SL105P

        Because RN7SL105P is a homologous pseudogene, short-read mapping may not reliably

distinguish it from related 7SL-derived sequences.

        No expression score should presently be assigned.
                                                                                   129

Phase 3C now permits the Phase 2/3A model to be reorganized by cellular compartment.

25. Hypothesis H1 — Membrane/Endosomal Co-Localization

       Original H1 predicted that multiple members of:
                                                                                       130

INPP5K + PITPNA + TLCD2 + WDR81 + RILP + SERPINF1

      would occupy at least one common retinal or visual-neural cell population.

Current result

      Partially supported, but substantially revised.

      A clear adult RPE module is emerging: INPP5K + PITPNA + SERPINF1.

      TLCD2 may instead preferentially involve cones.

      WDR81 and RILP are not presently shown to occupy the same adult retinal population.

      Therefore the original six-gene same-cell model is:

      not supported as a single adult retinal module.

      The broader pathway may instead span multiple adjacent cellular compartments.

26. Hypothesis H2 — Direct Retinal Localization

      The pre-specified candidates were:

PRPF8

SERPINF1

      RTN4RL1.

PRPF8

      Strongly supported.

      Broad neural-retinal and photoreceptor expression.

SERPINF1

      Strongly supported.

      Strong replicated RPE localization.

RTN4RL1

      Partially supported.
                                                                                                131

       Bulk retinal expression exists, but the specific adult retinal cell population remains

unresolved.

       Therefore H2 is:

supported for PRPF8 and SERPINF1, unresolved at cell-type level for

       RTN4RL1.

27. Hypothesis H3 — WDR81/RILP Neural Trafficking Overlap

       In adult retina:

       not currently supported.

       The two genes do not presently demonstrate convincing shared retinal cell-type

enrichment.

       This is one of Phase 3C's most important negative results.

       The appropriate next test is not to rescue the hypothesis by changing the criterion.

       It is to move the same frozen hypothesis into:

human dLGN

       and: human V1.

28. Hypothesis H6 — Regulatory Overlap

       PRPF8 is broadly present through neural retina and therefore has cellular opportunity to

influence transcripts in multiple retinal populations.

       The miRNA component cannot yet be evaluated because mature miR-22 requires a

different sequencing modality.

       Thus: PRPF8 overlap: spatially plausible. MIR22 regulatory overlap: unresolved.

       No direct target relationship should yet be inferred.
                                                                                                           132

30. Implication for the Original Visual-Neurological Question

Phase 3C strengthens the possibility of a retinal biological contribution, but not in the

         simplistic form initially conceivable.

         Current evidence does not suggest:

all deleted neural genes are simultaneously weakened inside the same retinal

         neuron.

         Instead it suggests a distributed system:

RPE molecular reserve

         ↓
                                                                                                134

       supports
                                 Distributed visual-system sequence
photoreceptor physiology

       ↓                                RPE molecular reserve


       feeds into
                                       Photoreceptor physiology
retinal neuronal circuitry

       ↓                               Retinal neuronal circuitry

       which ultimately projects through: RGC → dLGN → V1.
                                         RGC -> dLGN -> V1
       Different deleted genes may operate at different positions along that sequence.

       This is potentially more biologically realistic than a single-cell, single-pathway model.

       It also makes later visual-pathway analysis essential.

31. Important Negative Findings

Phase 3C currently provides no strong evidence for:

       RTN4RL1-specific enrichment in retinal ganglion cells.

       It provides no strong evidence that: WDR81 and RILP form a coexpressed adult-retinal

trafficking module.

       It provides no evidence that: SERPINF2 is a significant retinal gene.

       It provides no convincing specialized retinal localization for: SMYD4.

       And conventional scRNA-seq cannot resolve: mature MIR22 dosage.

       These negative results should be retained in the frozen dataset rather than discarded.

33. Evidence Boundary

Phase 3C now permits statements such as:

INPP5K, PITPNA and SERPINF1 show convergent expression within the

         RPE compartment.

         It permits:

PRPF8 is broadly expressed across human neural retina, including

         photoreceptors.

         It tentatively permits:

         TLCD2 may be enhanced in cone photoreceptors.

         It does not permit:

         The deletion impairs RPE function.

         It does not permit:

         The deletion damages cones.

         It does not permit:
                                                                                                137

          The RPE module causes visual stress.

          And it does not permit:

          The genes interact epistatically.

          Expression establishes:

          cellular opportunity for a mechanism.

          It does not establish pathological execution of that mechanism.

34. Phase 3C Working Conclusion

          Adult human retinal single-cell analysis substantially refines the bulk-expression model

developed in Phase 3A.

          The strongest finding is a reproducible RPE-centered convergence involving

SERPINF1, INPP5K, and PITPNA. These genes independently regulate extracellular

retinal

          trophic signaling, phosphoinositide metabolism, and phospholipid handling, respectively.

Their shared RPE localization provides the first strong evidence that multiple deleted genes with

related membrane/lipid functions occupy the same biologically relevant human ocular cell

compartment.

          PRPF8 represents a second major retinal axis. Rather than being narrowly restricted to

rods, current evidence supports broad expression through the neural retina, including rods and

cones, consistent with a fundamental spliceosomal function.

          TLCD2 provides a preliminary cone-photoreceptor signal that may extend the

membranelipid mechanism into neural retina, but replication is required.

          Conversely, the expected WDR81–RILP endosomal pair is not currently supported as a

shared adult retinal module. WDR81 shows a more microglial-associated expression pattern,
                                                                                                 138

whereas RILP lacks strong retina-specific enrichment and retains a broader neuronal profile.

RTN4RL1 also lacks demonstrated retinal ganglion-cell enrichment despite its strong

experimental optic-nerve biology.

Phase 3C therefore supports retinal involvement while narrowing and reorganizing

       the mechanism.

       The strongest present model is no longer:

       one six-gene membrane pathway operating uniformly throughout retina.

       It is:

an RPE-centered membrane/phosphoinositide/trophic module, a separate

photoreceptor-associated RNA/lipid module, and additional neural-

trafficking genes whose principal relevance may emerge farther downstream

       in the central visual pathway or during development.

       That distinction should now be carried forward unchanged into the next analyses.

Phase 3D — Human Dorsal Lateral Geniculate Nucleus Analysis

1. Objective

Phase 3D tests whether the loci affected by the 17p13.3 deletion show preferential

       localization within one of the principal human dorsal lateral geniculate nucleus visual

relay systems:

magnocellular — M

parvocellular — P

       or: koniocellular — K.

       This analysis directly addresses one of the project's pre-specified exploratory hypotheses:
                                                                                              139

H5 — One or more affected loci may show preferential expression in human

         magnocellular dLGN neurons.

         The analysis is deliberately symmetric.

         Evidence favoring P or K neurons would be retained with equal weight, and absence of

pathway-specific enrichment is treated as a meaningful negative result.

2. Biological Context

         The dorsal lateral geniculate nucleus is the principal thalamic relay between:

retina

         ↓

dLGN

         ↓ primary visual cortex — V1.

         In primates, visual information has traditionally been divided into magnocellular,

parvocellular and koniocellular streams.

         These populations differ substantially in:

   morphology;

   receptive-field properties;

   temporal response;

   contrast sensitivity;

   chromatic processing;

   retinal input;

   cortical projection patterns.

         The transcriptomic question is whether those physiological distinctions are accompanied

by correspondingly strong differences in gene expression.
                                                                                               140

        Current human evidence indicates that the answer is only partly yes.

3. Primary Human Dataset

        The principal dataset is the Allen Institute/Bakken et al. Comparative LGN study.

        The Allen Institute currently provides processed human LGN expression and metadata

for 1,209 nuclei. ("Comparative LGN")

        The published analysis used approximately 950 neuronal nuclei from three postmortem

human donors to construct the principal neuronal taxonomy. The study identified:

   three GABAergic neuronal populations;

   two koniocellular populations;

   one combined magnocellular/parvocellular projection-neuron population.

        The two koniocellular populations were designated:

Kap

        and: Kp.

        The remaining major glutamatergic population was designated:

MP. (Bakken et al.)

        This point is central to interpretation.

4. Human M and P Neurons Do Not Form Cleanly Separated Transcriptomic Classes

        Despite the well-established physiological distinction between magnocellular and

parvocellular neurons, human M and P nuclei did not resolve into two clean transcriptomic

clusters.

        Instead:

        M and P neurons largely formed a common MP cluster.

        Within that cluster, the investigators identified a continuous M-to-P expression gradient.
                                                                                           141

       Cross-species integration with macaque data confirmed that the human ends of this

continuum correspond to more clearly separable macaque M and P populations. (Bakken et al.)

       Therefore:

M versus P

       in adult human dLGN is better represented transcriptomically as:

a graded molecular distinction

       rather than:

       two strongly separated gene-expression states.

       This places an important constraint on the original magnocellular hypothesis.

5. Published Human M-versus-P Marker Test

       The study identified 32 genes showing consistent M-versus-P enrichment across all three

human donors.

       Validated examples included:

Magnocellular-associated

CRH

SUSD2

       with:

ROBO2

       also marking the M-directed end of the conserved human/macaque continuum.

      The complete published Figure 3 marker set includes genes such as:

6. None of the 17 Deleted Loci Is a Published M/P Marker

         The affected loci are:

         **INPP5K

         PITPNA

         SLC43A2

         SCARF1
                                                                                        144

       RILP

       PRPF8

       TLCD2

       WDR81

       SERPINF2

       SERPINF1

       SMYD4

       RPA1

       RTN4RL1

       PITPNA-AS1

       MIR22HG

       MIR22

       RN7SL105P.**

       None appears among the published genes showing the consistent human M-versus-P

marker pattern.

       Therefore:

No affected locus currently demonstrates published evidence of preferential

       magnocellular transcriptional enrichment in the adult human dLGN.

       Equally:

       No affected locus emerges as a strong published parvocellular marker.

       This is a genuine negative result for the pre-specified analysis.

7. This Does Not Mean the Genes Are Absent From M or P Neurons

       The distinction is critical.
                                                                                                145

       A gene can be expressed in:

both M and P neurons

       without being: differentially expressed between M and P neurons.

       For example, a housekeeping or broadly neuronal protein could be essential to both

populations while never appearing in an M-versus-P marker list.

       Therefore the result supports:

no demonstrated M-specific enrichment

       but does not support:

       no expression in magnocellular neurons.

       Those are different propositions.

8. Magnocellular Hypothesis Status

       The frozen Phase 3 hypothesis was:

       One or more affected genes may show preferential expression in human

       magnocellular dLGN neurons.

       The current result is: H5 — Not supported at the published marker level.

       None of the affected loci appears among the consistent human M-enriched markers.

       Therefore the project should not currently claim that the deletion preferentially targets the

magnocellular pathway.

       This substantially narrows the original hypothesis.

9. Why This Result Is Particularly Important

       A weaker analytical strategy could have proceeded from:

visual stress

       ↓
                                                                                               146

       historical magnocellular hypotheses

       ↓

       search for any deleted neural gene

       ↓

       claim magnocellular involvement.

Phase 3D does not permit that inference.

       When the actual human M/P transcriptomic dataset is examined first, the affected genes

do not appear among the genes distinguishing M from P neurons.

       Thus:

current transcriptomic evidence does not support a magnocellular-specific

       mechanism for this deletion.

       This is one of the clearest examples so far of the project's phenotype-neutral design

protecting against confirmation bias.

10. Koniocellular Analysis

       Human dLGN transcriptomics resolves the K populations more clearly than M and P.

       The Bakken et al. study identified:

Kap

       and:

Kp

       koniocellular types.

       The principal reported K-associated markers include:
                                                                                              147

CALB1

CAMK2A

PRKCG

       with:

PENK

       selectively identifying the Kp population.

       PENK-positive Kp neurons were anatomically confirmed in posterior K1 and K2 layers

using RNA in situ hybridization. (Bakken et al.)

11. No Deleted Locus Is a Defining K Marker

       None of the 17 affected loci is among the primary K-defining markers reported by the

human study.

       Thus the current evidence also fails to support:

       selective koniocellular involvement.

       At the transcriptomic-marker level, the deletion does not map specifically onto:

M

P

       or:

K

       identity.
