13. RILP

       RILP was specifically important entering Phase 3D because Phase 3C failed to establish a

strong adult retinal localization despite compelling neuronal trafficking biology.

       The dLGN analysis does not rescue RILP as a magnocellular-specific candidate.

       RILP is not among the published:

   M markers;

   P markers;

   K markers.

       Thus its neuronal trafficking mechanism may still operate in visual-relay neurons, but

current evidence does not associate it preferentially with one dLGN visual channel.

       This leaves RILP as a:

       general neural-trafficking candidate rather than a magnocellular candidate.
                                                                                           154

14. WDR81

       The same conclusion applies to WDR81.

Phase 2 established strong neural-development and endosomal PI3P biology.

Phase 3C suggested that adult retinal expression may be more glial than neuronal.

Phase 3D finds no evidence that WDR81 is a defining:

   magnocellular;

   parvocellular;

   koniocellular

       transcriptomic marker.

       Therefore:

WDR81 remains a strong neural mechanism but not a visual-relay-class-

       specific mechanism.

       Its strongest remaining Phase 3 opportunity may lie in:

   developmental brain;

   broader thalamic neurons;

   cortical populations.

15. RTN4RL1

       RTN4RL1 was another important candidate because Phase 2 established direct axonal

and optic-nerve relevance.

       However, RTN4RL1 also fails to appear among the published M/P/K defining gene sets.

       Therefore its functions involving:

   axonal growth;

   CSPG recognition;
                                                                                               155

   synaptic structural regulation

       should currently be interpreted as:

general neuronal structural biology

       rather than evidence of a magnocellular pathway specialization.

       This is consistent with Phase 3C, where retinal ganglion-cell-specific enrichment was

also not demonstrated.

       Two independent Phase 3 analyses therefore now fail to support a highly specialized

RTN4RL1 visual-channel localization.

16. PRPF8

       PRPF8 likewise shows no M/P/K marker specificity.

       This is biologically unsurprising.

       Its function is fundamental spliceosomal RNA processing.

Phase 3C demonstrated broad neural-retinal availability rather than narrow retinal

       subtype restriction.

Phase 3D now supports the same general interpretation centrally:

PRPF8 is more plausibly a broadly required cellular factor than a

       transcriptomic marker of a specific visual relay stream.

       This does not weaken PRPF8's retinal relevance.

       It weakens only a hypothesis of magnocellular-selective PRPF8 vulnerability.

17. PITPNA, INPP5K, TLCD2 and SERPINF1

       The Phase 3C retinal lipid module does not reappear as an M-specific dLGN

transcriptional module.

       None of:
                                                                                            156

PITPNA

INPP5K

TLCD2

       or:

SERPINF1

       is among the published human M/P discriminating genes.

       Therefore the strongest Phase 3C finding remains principally:

retinal/RPE biology

       rather than:

       magnocellular thalamic biology.

       This begins to spatially separate the two hypotheses.

18. A Critical Property of the Human dLGN Dataset

       Failure to find M-specific enrichment should not be exaggerated.

       The original study itself found that human M and P neurons are remarkably similar

transcriptomically relative to their major physiological and morphological differences.

       Only a limited set of genes consistently distinguishes the two.

       The human M/P cells remain one MP transcriptomic population with a molecular

gradient rather than completely distinct cell types. (Bakken et al.)

       Consequently:

physiological specialization need not require large differences in steady-state

       gene expression.

       A deleted gene expressed equally in M and P neurons could theoretically have different

functional consequences if the two neuronal types impose different physiological demands.
                                                                                          157

       That possibility remains open.

       However, there is currently no positive transcriptomic evidence supporting it.

19. Independent Primate Evidence Supports the Complexity of LGN Cell Identity

       A 2026 reanalysis of the Allen macaque LGN dataset identified additional heterogeneity

within the conventional M/P/K categories:

   two magnocellular subpopulations;

   two parvocellular subpopulations;

   three koniocellular subpopulations.

       The analysis used 1,309 macaque LGN nuclei and found substantial within-class

transcriptomic complexity. (Sun, Renshaw, and Pezaris)

       This reinforces an important conceptual point:

       M, P and K are themselves not molecularly homogeneous populations.

       The recent macaque analysis does not highlight RTN4RL1, RILP, WDR81, PRPF8,

PITPNA, INPP5K, SERPINF1, TLCD2, or RPA1 among the principal subtype genes discussed

in the article. (Sun, Renshaw, and Pezaris)

       This is supporting primate evidence only.

       It does not replace the human analysis.

20. Revised Interpretation of the Magnocellular Hypothesis

       At the start of the project, one conceivable mechanism was:

17p13.3 deletion

       ↓

       specific disruption of genes important to the magnocellular pathway

       ↓
                                                                                            158

       altered temporal/contrast processing

       ↓

       candidate visual-stress phenotype.

Phase 3D does not support the critical middle step.

       The current evidence instead favors:

17p13.3 deletion

       ↓

       multiple retinal and broadly neuronal molecular perturbations

       ↓

       possible visual-system vulnerability

       without evidence of: preferential magnocellular transcriptional targeting.

       This is a substantially more conservative and defensible model.

21. Consequence for the Visual-Stress Hypothesis

       The historical literature around visual stress has sometimes invoked magnocellular

dysfunction.

Phase 3D means that this project should not use that literature as a shortcut to connect

       the CNV with visual stress.

       At present:

The genomic findings do not independently predict a magnocellular-specific

       lesion.

       If later physiological evidence were to implicate magnocellular processing, that would

require a separate explanation.

       The present transcriptomic evidence does not supply it.
                                                                              159

22. What Phase 3D Does Support

Phase 3D does support a different conclusion.

        Several of the affected genes have substantial:

   neural;

   retinal;

   membrane;

   trafficking;

   signaling

        functions.

        But those mechanisms appear more likely to operate as:

broad cellular systems

        than as molecular identities unique to one thalamic visual channel.

        This is especially consistent with:

PRPF8

                               → universal spliceosomal machinery

RPA1

                                → genome-maintenance machinery

RILP

                             → general neuronal endosomal transport

WDR81

                                  → general endosomal signaling

RTN4RL1

                             → broadly neuronal structural signaling.
                                                                                    160

23. Cross-Phase Comparison

Phase 3 has now produced three distinct spatial findings.

Phase 3A — Whole Retina

       Strong expression of:

       **PRPF8

       SERPINF1

       PITPNA

       RPA1

       INPP5K

       TLCD2**

       with weaker adult-retinal signals for several trafficking genes.

Phase 3C — Retinal Cell Types

       Strongest convergence:

INPP5K + PITPNA + SERPINF1

       within the:

RPE compartment

       with PRPF8 broadly available through neural retina and possible TLCD2 cone

association.

Phase 3D — dLGN

       No affected locus is currently identified as a strong:

M

P

       or:
                                                                                           161

K

       marker.

       Thus the most specific spatial convergence discovered so far remains:

       RPE-centered rather than magnocellular-centered.

24. Hypothesis Status After Phase 3D

H1 — Membrane/endosomal colocalization

       Partially supported, principally through RPE rather than dLGN subtype specialization.

H2 — Direct retinal localization

       Supported for PRPF8 and SERPINF1; strong RPE convergence with INPP5K/PITPNA.

H3 — WDR81/RILP neural-trafficking overlap

Not demonstrated in adult retina and not demonstrated as M/P/K-specific in human

       dLGN.

       Still open in cortical/developmental analyses.

H4 — Developmental enrichment

       Not yet tested.

H5 — Magnocellular specificity

       Not supported.

       This is now a meaningful negative result.

H6 — Regulatory overlap

       Still unresolved.
                                                                                                 162

25. Evidence Boundary

Phase 3D permits the statement:

None of the 17 affected loci has emerged as a published defining marker of

        adult human magnocellular, parvocellular, or koniocellular dLGN identity.

        It also permits:

The available human transcriptomic data do not support preferential

        magnocellular targeting by the deletion.

        It does not permit:

        The affected genes are absent from magnocellular neurons.

        Nor does it permit:

        Magnocellular physiology cannot be affected.

        Those stronger claims would require direct quantitative expression and physiological

data.

26. Technical Limitation

        The Allen Institute makes the complete processed human LGN expression matrix and

metadata publicly downloadable, containing 1,209 nuclei. ("Comparative LGN")

        The published paper and associated supplementary analyses provide the marker-level

M/P/K results used here, including the 32 reproducible human M/P genes and the defining K

markers. The underlying processed dataset is also deposited under GSE182211 and through the

Allen Comparative LGN resource. ("GSE182211"; "Comparative LGN")

        A final frozen Phase 3 matrix should ultimately include direct numerical extraction of

each affected gene's expression across individual M, P and K nuclei.

        Therefore the present conclusion is appropriately classified as:
                                                                                              163

strong marker-level negative evidence for subtype specificity

       rather than:

       complete quantitative absence of differential expression.

27. Phase 3D Working Conclusion

       Human dLGN transcriptomics does not currently support the hypothesis that the 17p13.3

microdeletion preferentially disrupts a magnocellular molecular program.

       Human magnocellular and parvocellular relay neurons themselves are transcriptionally

much more similar than their well-known physiological differences might suggest. They form a

largely shared MP transcriptomic population with a comparatively subtle M-to-P expression

gradient. A limited set of genes—including CRH, SUSD2 and ROBO2 toward the magnocellular

end and EPHA7 and FOXP2 toward the parvocellular end—defines this gradient. None of the 17

loci affected by the index deletion appears among the published reproducible M/P marker genes.

(Bakken et al.)

       Likewise, the affected loci do not overlap the principal human koniocellular markers

CALB1, CAMK2A, PRKCG and PENK.

       Accordingly:

The pre-specified magnocellular-specificity hypothesis is not supported by

       current adult human dLGN transcriptomic evidence.

       This result does not weaken the broader proposition that the deletion may affect

visualneural biology. Instead, it changes the likely mechanism.

       The strongest spatial evidence remains the RPE-centered INPP5K–PITPNA–
                                                                                                 164

SERPINF1 convergence, combined with broad retinal PRPF8 expression and additional

neural

         mechanisms that do not appear restricted to a particular thalamic visual stream.

         The current model should therefore shift from:

a magnocellular-specific defect

         toward:

a distributed retinal and neural functional-reserve model in which several

molecular systems may be altered without preferentially targeting one

         classical M/P/K visual channel.

         That is a stronger conclusion scientifically because it follows the transcriptomic evidence

rather than the original phenotype hypothesis.

Phase 3C - Integrated Expression and Spatiotemporal Analysis

         Research Scope and Status

Phase 3 evaluates where and when genes disrupted by the GRCh37/hg19

         chr17:1,411,408-1,841,103 microdeletion are expressed, with particular emphasis on the

retina, retinal pigment epithelium (RPE), dorsal lateral geniculate nucleus (dLGN), primary

visual cortex (V1), and developing human neocortex. The phase does not treat expression as

evidence of disease causation. Its purpose is narrower: to determine whether genes already

identified as structurally disrupted and mechanistically plausible in Phases 1 and 2 have spatial

and temporal opportunity to influence visual-neural biology.

         The deletion interval contains thirteen protein-coding loci considered in the Phase 3

expression analysis (INPP5K, PITPNA, SLC43A2, SCARF1, RILP, PRPF8, TLCD2, WDR81,

SERPINF2, SERPINF1, SMYD4, RPA1, and RTN4RL1) and four noncoding annotations
                                                                                                165

(PITPNA-AS1, MIR22HG, MIR22, and RN7SL105P). Phase 3 preserves the structural

interpretation established previously: the deletion begins within INPP5K and ends within

RTN4RL1, with the latter losing essentially the canonical coding sequence on the affected allele.

Those structural facts are background constraints rather than expression results.

Abstract

Phase 3 integrates bulk tissue expression, single-cell and single-nucleus transcriptomics,

       retinal cell-type resources, human dLGN transcriptomics, adult human brain atlases,

developmental cortical multiomics, and spatial fetal-cortex transcriptomics. The strongest direct

cell-localized convergence remains retinal and RPE-centered: INPP5K and PITPNA show

RPEassociated expression, while SERPINF1 is strongly associated with retinal pigment

epithelial cells and has an established retinal trophic context. Human Protein Atlas data classify

INPP5K as group-enriched in retinal pigment epithelial cells and PITPNA as cell-type enhanced

in retinal pigment epithelial cells; SERPINF1 is likewise group-enriched in retinal pigment

epithelial cells (Human Protein Atlas, “INPP5K”; Human Protein Atlas, “PITPNA”; Human

Protein Atlas, “SERPINF1”). PRPF8 is broadly expressed in retina rather than restricted to one

visual cell class, TLCD2 has a cone-photoreceptor signal that remains preliminary for

mechanistic interpretation, WDR81 is more compatible with microglial/non-specific cellular

expression than a mature retinal neuronal module, and RTN4RL1 is detectably expressed in

human retina but has not been demonstrated as retinal-ganglion-cell-specific (Human Protein

Atlas, “TLCD2”; Human Protein Atlas, “WDR81”; Human Protein Atlas, “RTN4RL1”).

       Central visual-system analysis does not support a magnocellular-specific model.

Singlecell and single-nucleus profiling of primate dLGN found that human magnocellular and

parvocellular projection neurons are transcriptomically similar relative to their marked
                                                                                                   166

physiological differences; the affected 17p13.3 loci do not emerge as defining published M-, P-,

or K-cell markers (Bakken et al.). Adult cortical analysis similarly supports distributed neural

expression rather than a deletion-wide V1-specific signature. Developmental analysis strengthens

evidence that selected genes have temporal opportunity during cortical development, especially

WDR81, RTN4RL1, RILP, and SERPINF1, but independent human developmental atlases

demonstrate readily detectable V1 specialization without identifying the deletion interval as a

conspicuous V1-defining program (Bhaduri et al.; Wang et al.; Qian et al.). The resulting model

is therefore one of distributed functional reserve: several deleted genes plausibly constrain

membrane, phosphoinositide, endosomal, trophic, RNA-processing, or genome-maintenance

systems at different visual-system sites and developmental stages, but the complete causal chain

from heterozygous dosage loss to biochemical disturbance to visual-neural phenotype has not

been demonstrated.

       Methods and Evidentiary Rules

Phase 3 was designed as an expression-opportunity analysis rather than a phenotype-

       association screen. Evidence was interpreted in the following order: genomic disruption,

known molecular function, tissue or cell-type expression, spatiotemporal overlap, pathway

convergence, perturbational evidence, phenotype association, and causality last. Accordingly,

detection of a transcript in retina or cortex establishes only that the gene is biologically available

in that compartment. It does not establish that the heterozygous deletion changes transcript

abundance, protein abundance, enzymatic flux, synaptic physiology, or perception.

       Adult tissue-level observations were treated as lower-resolution evidence than

cellresolved data. Single-cell and single-nucleus findings were prioritized when the relevant cell

type was represented and assay limitations were understood. Developmental comparisons were
                                                                                                 167

not interpreted as longitudinal trajectories unless the underlying study design supported that

inference. Cross-sectional BrainSpan or Allen-derived standardized expression values from

different donors and ages were therefore treated as suggestive temporal observations rather than

within-subject fold changes. BrainSpan provides RNA-seq and microarray expression data

across multiple cortical and subcortical structures and developmental periods, including prenatal

laser-microdissection resources (Allen Institute for Brain Science).

       Noncoding loci required additional caution. Standard polyadenylated or nuclear RNA

workflows are not reliable assays for mature microRNA abundance; consequently, MIR22

cannot be considered absent or low merely because it is poorly represented in ordinary

singlenucleus RNA-seq. MIR22HG and MIR22 were also treated as related host/microRNA

annotations rather than independent deletion events. PITPNA-AS1 and RN7SL105P remain

technically underdetermined in the available visual-system single-cell resources.

       Evidence labels used throughout this phase are: established, when supported directly by

the relevant human dataset or well-validated cell-resolved resource; suggestive, when supported

by cross-sectional, lower-resolution, or indirect evidence; inference, when a mechanistic

interpretation combines independent observations without direct experimental demonstration;

and unsupported, when the proposed specificity or relationship is not demonstrated by the

reviewed evidence.

Phase 3A: Adult Whole-Tissue and Ocular Expression

       The initial adult expression screen asked whether the affected genes were detectably

expressed in retina and brain before attempting cell-type localization. This screen supported

substantial adult retinal expression opportunity for PRPF8, SERPINF1, PITPNA, RPA1,

INPP5K, and TLCD2, with lower or less discriminatory whole-retina signals for several other
                                                                                                168

interval genes. Whole-tissue expression was not treated as evidence that all of these genes act in

the same retinal cell. The Human Protein Atlas retina resource illustrates the interpretive

problem: the retina expresses a large fraction of the human proteome, so bulk detection alone has

limited specificity (Human Protein Atlas, “Retina Proteome”).

       RTN4RL1 is a useful example. Current Human Protein Atlas data report detectable

retinal RNA, with a GTEx-derived consensus around 7.9 nTPM, supporting retinal opportunity

but not assigning that expression to retinal ganglion cells or another specific neuronal population

(Human Protein Atlas, “RTN4RL1”). TLCD2 likewise shows retinal RNA in bulk data, while its

single-cell annotation includes cone photoreceptor enhancement; this makes cone involvement a

reasonable candidate for follow-up, not an established cone-specific deletion mechanism

(Human Protein Atlas, “TLCD2”).

Phase 3A therefore established a permissive rather than causal result: multiple affected

       genes are present in adult ocular tissue, so retinal mechanisms cannot be excluded on

expression grounds. The phase then moved to cell-resolved localization because bulk retinal

abundance could not distinguish photoreceptor, RPE, glial, vascular, immune, or inner-retinal

contributions.

Phase 3B-3C: Retinal Cell Types and RPE-Centered Convergence

       Human retinal single-cell resources provide a substantially stronger anatomical test.

Cowan and colleagues sequenced 285,441 single cells from retinal organoids across

developmental time points and from adult human peripheral retina, fovea, pigment epithelium,

and choroid, demonstrating that retinal disease-related genes can show marked cell-type

specificity (Cowan et al.). Independent adult retinal atlases likewise resolve foveal and peripheral
                                                                                                     169

retinal cell populations at single-cell scale (Yan et al.). These datasets make it possible to ask

whether mechanistically related deletion genes actually occupy compatible retinal compartments.

       The strongest Phase 3 retinal result is the RPE-centered convergence of INPP5K,

PITPNA, and SERPINF1. Human Protein Atlas single-cell annotations classify INPP5K as

group-enriched in retinal pigment epithelial cells and place it in an RPE-associated expression

cluster. PITPNA is cell-type enhanced in RPE, while SERPINF1 is group-enriched in RPE and

decidual stromal cells (Human Protein Atlas, “INPP5K”; Human Protein Atlas, “PITPNA”;

Human Protein Atlas, “SERPINF1”). This is important because the three genes are not merely

co-expressed names: their known molecular functions occupy complementary membrane, lipid,

phosphoinositide, and trophic biology. Phase 3 does not claim that they form a single

biochemical complex or that their heterozygous loss produces a measured RPE defect. It

establishes spatial opportunity for functional convergence.

       PRPF8 presents a different pattern. It is broadly expressed in retina and is biologically

relevant to retinal disease under established variant mechanisms, but its expression is not

restricted to one RPE or photoreceptor subtype. Consequently, PRPF8 is best treated as a broadly

available RNA-processing constraint rather than evidence for the RPE membrane module. Its

presence may matter if future perturbational work demonstrates altered splicing in affected visual

cells, but expression alone cannot supply that missing step.

       TLCD2 provides a weaker but potentially informative signal. Current Human Protein

Atlas single-cell annotation lists cone photoreceptor cells among TLCD2-enhanced cell types,

while bulk retina RNA is detectable (Human Protein Atlas, “TLCD2”). This supports a

preliminary cone-associated expression opportunity. It does not establish that TLCD2 dosage
                                                                                              170

affects cone membrane composition in the deletion carrier, nor that such an effect contributes to

photophobia, pattern glare, color processing, or another visual phenotype.

          WDR81 did not produce the expected mature retinal neuronal convergence with RILP.

Human Protein Atlas annotations describe WDR81 as having low overall cell-type specificity

with enhancement in microglia among several cell types (Human Protein Atlas, “WDR81”).

RILP, by contrast, is assigned to a neuronal neurotransmission-and-signaling expression cluster

in the broader single-cell atlas (Human Protein Atlas, “RILP”). These findings do not

demonstrate a shared adult retinal neuronal trafficking module. The WDR81-RILP relationship

therefore remained mechanistically plausible from Phase 2 but anatomically unresolved in adult

retina.

          The retinal conclusion is correspondingly asymmetric. INPP5K-PITPNA-SERPINF1

represents the strongest direct cell-localized convergence in Phase 3. PRPF8 contributes broad

retinal RNA-processing opportunity; TLCD2 has preliminary cone-associated support;

RTN4RL1 is expressed in retina but lacks demonstrated retinal-ganglion-cell enrichment; and

WDR81-RILP co-localization in mature retinal neurons has not been established.

Phase 3D: Human dLGN and the Magnocellular Hypothesis

Phase 3D tested whether the deletion genes preferentially map to the magnocellular

          visual pathway. This was a particularly important falsification test because a

magnocellular model could otherwise become an attractive but weakly constrained explanation

for visual discomfort or contrast-related symptoms. Bakken and colleagues profiled primate

dLGN using single-cell and single-nucleus RNA sequencing, including nuclei from anatomically

defined human magnocellular, parvocellular, and koniocellular regions. They identified four

principal thalamocortical neuron types in primates but found relatively few strong transcriptional
                                                                                                171

differences between M and P populations despite their well-known morphological and

physiological differences (Bakken et al.).

       In human dLGN, M and P neurons largely occupy a shared transcriptomic MP cluster

with a gradient rather than two completely separate molecular classes. Published M-associated

markers include CRH and SUSD2, whereas EPHA7 is among validated P-associated markers;

the study also identifies K-cell classes separately (Bakken et al.). None of the affected 17p13.3

loci emerged as a defining published M, P, or principal K marker in this analysis.

       This is a negative result with a specific meaning. It does not show that the deletion genes

are absent from magnocellular neurons. It shows that the available human dLGN transcriptomic

evidence does not support preferential magnocellular enrichment. Because M and P neurons are

themselves transcriptomically similar, absence from a marker list cannot be treated as proof of

equal functional importance in both populations. Nevertheless, the data fail to support a

deletionwide magnocellular-specific mechanism. Hypothesis H5 is therefore classified as

unsupported.

Phase 3E: Adult Human V1 and Central Cortical Distribution

Phase 3E extended the analysis from the thalamus to human cerebral cortex, with

       particular interest in V1. Large adult human brain atlases demonstrate extensive neuronal

and glial diversity across the brain and provide a framework for distinguishing broadly expressed

genes from regionally specialized programs. Siletti and colleagues profiled more than three

million nuclei from approximately one hundred dissections and identified thousands of

transcriptomic subclusters, including area-specific cortical neurons (Siletti et al.).

       Within this framework, several affected genes have credible central-neural expression,

but the interval does not emerge as a coherent adult V1-specific program. RILP gains support as
                                                                                                   172

a neuronal gene because the Human Protein Atlas places it in a neuronal neurotransmission-

andsignaling expression cluster (Human Protein Atlas, “RILP”). RTN4RL1 has human

cerebralcortex and retinal expression and is classified as tissue enriched in cerebral cortex in

current Human Protein Atlas protein-summary metadata, but this does not establish V1 or

cortical-layer specificity (Human Protein Atlas, “RTN4RL1”). PITPNA and INPP5K remain

broadly available rather than demonstrably V1-selective.

       SERPINF1 retains trophic and vascular/glial opportunities in central tissue but does not

reproduce the same sharply localized RPE pattern in V1. WDR81 and RILP remain compatible

with endosomal and neuronal trafficking biology, yet Phase 3E found no evidence that WDR81-

RILP-RTN4RL1 form a synchronized adult V1 cell-type module. PRPF8 and RPA1 are even

less regionally discriminatory because their core functions in RNA processing and genome

maintenance make broad expression biologically expected.

       The adult cortical result therefore favors distributed vulnerability over a simple

visualcortex lesion model. The genes can be biologically active in cortex without being

selectively enriched in V1, layer 4, intratelencephalic neurons, corticothalamic neurons, or

another single cortical class. This distinction became the rationale for Phase 3F: if adult

specificity was weak, developmental timing might still reveal a period during which the deleted

genes are unusually relevant to visual-cortex formation.

Phase 3F: Human Visual-Cortex Development and Developmental Enrichment

Phase 3F initially centered on the developmental multiomic atlas of Wang and colleagues

       and was subsequently broadened to independent cortical arealization and spatial-

transcriptomic resources. Wang et al. analyzed 27 brain specimens and 38 biological samples

spanning five developmental stages from the first trimester through adolescence. Paired single-
                                                                                                  173

nucleus RNAseq and ATAC-seq were generated from PFC and V1, producing 243,535 nuclei

after initial quality control and a final cortical dataset of approximately 232,000 nuclei. The

study resolved progenitor, neuronal, glial, immune, and vascular classes and explicitly modeled

regional developmental differences (Wang et al.).

       The atlas is particularly valuable because it identifies a V1-specific layer-4

intratelencephalic trajectory rather than assuming that all layer-4 neurons are equivalent.

Common and V1-specific EN-L4-IT populations share an early trajectory and diverge later in

development. The authors reported 1,908 differentially expressed genes distinguishing

V1specific from common EN-L4-IT neurons and highlighted markers such as CUX1 and

KCNIP1. Their analysis used sample-level pseudobulk logic with age and dataset effects and

Benjamini- Hochberg multiple-testing correction (Wang et al.). None of the affected 17p13.3

loci is among the conspicuous published markers defining that V1-specific branch.

       A crucial evidence boundary was maintained throughout the Table 18 investigation. The

supplementary archive containing the full differential-expression table is publicly listed, but the

exact per-gene rows for all affected loci were not successfully retrieved in the research

environment. Consequently, Phase 3 does not label the deletion genes statistically nonsignificant

in that comparison. The justified statement is narrower: they are not among the highlighted

defining V1-specific EN-L4-IT markers, while their exact effect sizes and adjusted P values

remain an archival verification item.

       The broadened search materially strengthens the interpretation because independent

datasets show that V1 specialization is readily detectable when present. Bhaduri and colleagues

profiled ten major brain structures and six neocortical areas during peak neurogenesis and early

gliogenesis. They found extensive area-specific differential expression in the second trimester,
                                                                                               174

including in radial glia, with areal signatures becoming more pronounced as cells differentiate

toward excitatory neurons. Their results support strong frontal-versus-occipital molecular

patterning rather than a cortex that is transcriptionally homogeneous during development

(Bhaduri et al.).

        Qian and colleagues provide an even stronger spatial constraint. Using MERFISH and

complementary approaches, they constructed a spatial atlas exceeding 18 million fetal cortical

cells across eight cortical areas and seven developmental time points. They detected a discrete

V1-V2 molecular boundary by approximately gestational week 20 and found early

synaptogenesis-related changes in V1-specific layer-4 neurons (Qian et al.). Thus, the available

technology and datasets are capable of detecting visual-cortex-specific developmental programs.

The failure of the 17p13.3 interval to emerge as a conspicuous defining program cannot simply

be attributed to an inability of developmental transcriptomics to resolve V1. Developmental

Candidate: WDR81

        WDR81 remains one of the strongest early-development candidates in the interval, but

the evidence supports developmental opportunity rather than V1 specificity. Cross-sectional

developing-brain resources suggest prenatal cortical expression, and its established endosomal

biology gives that expression mechanistic relevance. However, WDR81 is not identified as a

major V1 arealization determinant in the Bhaduri, Wang, or Qian studies. The Phase 3F

classification is therefore: developmental neural relevance, suggestive to moderate; preferential

V1 specification, unsupported. Developmental Candidate: RTN4RL1

        RTN4RL1 is detectably expressed in human retina and brain and has a neuronal

cellsurface biology compatible with neurite and circuit maturation. BrainSpan and Allen

developmental resources provide temporal expression opportunity, while current Human Protein
                                                                                                  175

Atlas data show cerebral-cortex enrichment at the tissue level (Allen Institute for Brain Science;

Human Protein Atlas, “RTN4RL1”). The expression pattern is better described as

developmentally dynamic than as a simple prenatal peak. Because RTN4RL1 also suffers

nearcomplete canonical coding loss on the affected allele, it remains a high-priority mechanistic

candidate. Yet it is not established as a V1-specific arealization gene. Developmental Candidate:

RILP

       RILP retains a credible neuronal trafficking role but has weaker evidence for early

cortical specification. Its neuronal expression-cluster assignment supports central-neural

opportunity (Human Protein Atlas, “RILP”), while the developmental atlases do not identify it as

a major V1 arealization or layer-4 determinant. The most conservative interpretation is that RILP

may matter during neuronal maturation and endolysosomal cargo transport rather than during

initial specification of V1 identity. Developmental Candidate: SERPINF1

       SERPINF1 bridges the strongest retinal result and the developmental-cortical analysis. Its

RPE enrichment is direct, while a 2026 developmental-brain analysis reported a rapid increase in

SERPINF1 expression between approximately 24 gestational weeks and four months after birth

(Musante et al.). This observation is secondary to that study's primary disease question, so it

should not be over-weighted; nevertheless, it supports a developmental trophic opportunity

during the late-prenatal to early-postnatal transition. It does not establish V1 specificity.

Functional Ordering without a Demonstrated Molecular Chain

       The broadened developmental evidence permits a cautious temporal functional model:

WDR81 is most compatible with earlier endosomal and developmental regulation; RTN4RL1

with later structural, neurite, and circuit-maturation opportunity; and RILP with neuronal

endolysosomal transport during maturation. This ordering is an inference assembled from
                                                                                                176

independent functional and expression observations. It is not evidence that one gene activates the

next, that the genes form a shared transcriptional module, or that heterozygous loss produces a

sequential biochemical cascade.

       Wang et al. also inferred hundreds of enhancer-associated regulatory programs from

paired RNA and chromatin-accessibility data. The presence of a mapped regulatory landscape

makes a shared deletion-specific V1 regulon testable in principle. At present, however, no

published result establishes that the affected genes constitute a coordinated V1-specific

eRegulon. Regulatory hypothesis H6 therefore remains technically testable but unsupported at

the deletion-module level.

       Integrated Gene-Level Interpretation

       INPP5K. Strong RPE localization opportunity; phosphoinositide biology makes the RPE

finding mechanistically coherent. Developmental cortical expression is compatible with a

broader role, but V1 specificity is not demonstrated. Evidence status: established RPE expression

opportunity; developmental regional specificity unsupported.

       PITPNA. RPE-enhanced expression and neuronal/synaptic biology support

membranelipid opportunity in retina and brain. The strongest Phase 3 use of PITPNA is as part

of the RPEcentered convergence, not as a V1 marker. Evidence status: established RPE

opportunity; V1 specificity unsupported.

       SLC43A2. Expression opportunity is broad and not strongly visual-system-specific in the

reviewed Phase 3 resources. Evidence status: low discriminatory value for Phase 3.

       SCARF1. The available expression pattern is more compatible with immune/vascular

biology than a specialized retinal-neuronal or V1 program. Evidence status: low support for a

direct visual-neuronal localization hypothesis.
                                                                                               177

       RILP. Central neuronal expression is supported, and its trafficking function remains

mechanistically relevant. Adult retinal neuronal co-localization with WDR81 and developmental

V1 specificity are not demonstrated. Evidence status: moderate neuronal opportunity; specific

visual module unresolved.

       PRPF8. Broad retinal and neural expression is expected for a core spliceosomal factor. It

remains biologically important but spatially nondiscriminatory. Evidence status: strong

expression opportunity; low regional specificity.

       TLCD2. Bulk retinal expression and cone-photoreceptor enhancement provide a

preliminary cell-type lead. Evidence status: suggestive cone-associated opportunity; mechanistic

and dosage consequences untested.

       WDR81. Developmental and central-neural opportunity is supported, with microglial

enhancement in current single-cell annotations. It remains a strong developmental candidate

because of independent endosomal biology, not because it is a V1-specific marker. Evidence

status: moderate developmental relevance; V1 specificity unsupported.

       SERPINF2. Phase 3 provides little evidence for a specialized visual-neural expression

pattern. Evidence status: low visual-system discriminatory value.

       SERPINF1. Strong RPE enrichment and late-prenatal/early-postnatal developmental

expression dynamics make SERPINF1 one of the most spatially informative genes in the

interval. Evidence status: strong retinal/RPE opportunity; developmental trophic opportunity

supported; V1 specificity unsupported.

       SMYD4. Broad/low-specificity expression provides little anatomical discrimination.

Evidence status: low Phase 3 specificity.
                                                                                               178

       RPA1. Broad expression is consistent with genome-maintenance function. Its importance

is more likely to emerge through dosage-sensitive cellular stress than visual-system localization.

Evidence status: strong general cellular opportunity; low visual specificity.

       RTN4RL1. Retinal and cortical expression plus severe allelic coding disruption make

RTN4RL1 a high-priority neuronal candidate. Cell-surface and circuit-maturation biology are

compatible with visual-neural relevance, but retinal-ganglion-cell, V1-layer, and magnocellular

specificity remain unproven. Evidence status: moderate-to-high neural opportunity; regional

specificity unsupported.

       PITPNA-AS1 / MIR22HG / MIR22 / RN7SL105P. Available Phase 3 assays are

inadequate for confident visual-system localization of these noncoding loci, especially mature

MIR22. Evidence status: technically underdetermined; no negative inference from ordinary

snRNA-seq is permitted.

       Hypothesis Adjudication

       H1: Membrane/endosomal colocalization. Partially supported. The strongest celllocalized

evidence is the RPE-centered INPP5K-PITPNA-SERPINF1 configuration. A comparable

deletion-wide neuronal module has not been demonstrated in retina, dLGN, or V1.

       H2: Direct retinal localization. Supported for selected genes, especially INPP5K,

PITPNA, SERPINF1, PRPF8, TLCD2, and RTN4RL1 at differing levels of cellular resolution.

Direct localization does not establish pathological effect.

       H3: WDR81-RILP trafficking overlap. Mechanistically plausible but anatomically

unresolved. Adult retinal neuronal co-localization is not demonstrated; developmental evidence

is more compatible with temporally distributed roles.
                                                                                                 179

       H4: Developmental enrichment. Partially supported. Several genes have credible

developmental cortical opportunity, but the interval is not demonstrated as a V1-specific

developmental program.

       H5: Magnocellular specificity. Unsupported. Human dLGN transcriptomics does not

identify the affected loci as defining magnocellular markers, and no later developmental

evidence restores this hypothesis.

       H6: Shared V1 regulatory overlap. Presently unsupported. Modern multiomic atlases

make the question testable, but no shared affected-gene V1-specific eRegulon has been

established.

       Interpretive Synthesis: Developmental and Cellular Functional Reserve

       The most defensible lay and technical synthesis of Phase 3 is a functional-reserve model.

Several genes in the deletion are expressed in biologically relevant visual or neural

compartments, and some occupy complementary molecular systems. In RPE, the convergence is

unusually concrete because INPP5K, PITPNA, and SERPINF1 share cell-type opportunity while

contributing to phosphoinositide, lipid/membrane, and trophic biology. In the developing cortex,

WDR81, RTN4RL1, RILP, SERPINF1, PRPF8, and RPA1 provide different forms of

developmental or cellular opportunity, but not a single V1-specific transcriptional program.

       The term functional reserve is preferable to assuming a fixed fifty-percent loss of

function. A heterozygous deletion removes one genomic copy, but the downstream protein or

pathway effect depends on transcriptional compensation, protein stability, feedback regulation,

redundant pathways, cell type, developmental stage, and physiological demand. Phase 3

therefore supports the proposition that the deletion could reduce reserve in several molecular

systems; it does not quantify that reduction.
                                                                                              180

       Likewise, the project has not yet demonstrated a complete chemical or molecular chain

linking the deletion to a visual-neural phenotype. Individual biochemical functions are known:

INPP5K acts in phosphoinositide metabolism; PITPNA participates in

phosphatidylinositol/phosphatidylcholine transfer; WDR81 and RILP participate in

endosomal/lysosomal trafficking; SERPINF1/PEDF has trophic and lipid-linked retinal biology;

PRPF8 is a core spliceosomal component; RPA1 supports replication and genome maintenance;

and RTN4RL1 is a neuronal surface receptor-family protein. What remains unproven is the

connecting chain from heterozygous dosage reduction to altered pathway chemistry in the

relevant human visual cell, then to altered circuit function, and finally to a reproducible

phenotype.

       Negative Findings and Falsification Value

Phase 3 generated several negative findings that materially constrain the research. First,

       the deletion does not presently support a magnocellular-specific model. Second, adult V1

does not reveal a deletion-wide region- or layer-specific expression program. Third, the mature

retinal WDR81-RILP trafficking pair was not demonstrated as a shared neuronal module. Fourth,

the developmental interval genes do not emerge among the conspicuous V1-specific layer-4

markers in modern human developmental atlases. Fifth, no shared V1-specific regulatory

network among the affected genes has been established.

       These negative findings are not failures of the project. They reduce the number of

plausible mechanistic models and prevent the analysis from drifting toward a preferred

phenotype narrative. The strongest surviving model is more distributed: retinal/RPE

membranetrophic vulnerability combined with central-neural developmental, trafficking, RNA-
                                                                                                181

processing, and genome-maintenance constraints. This model is less anatomically simple but

better aligned with the evidence.

       Limitations

       The principal limitation is that expression is not perturbation. None of the reviewed

atlases directly measures the molecular state of cells carrying this specific heterozygous deletion.

Normal-reference expression establishes opportunity, not dosage response. A gene may be

highly expressed yet fully compensated after heterozygous loss, or modestly expressed yet

functionally dose-sensitive.

       Second, cell-resolved atlases have sampling and technical limitations. Single-nucleus

methods can underrepresent cytoplasmic transcripts, rare cell populations can be missed, and

regional dissections are imperfect. The dLGN study itself notes practical limitations in isolating

thin K layers (Bakken et al.). Spatial assays solve some localization problems but often measure

restricted gene panels or snapshots rather than complete developmental trajectories.

       Third, developmental datasets are generally cross-sectional across different donors.

Apparent age-related changes should not be interpreted as within-person trajectories unless the

analysis explicitly models age across samples. The older Allen/BrainSpan signals used in Phase

3F were therefore treated as supportive context, not definitive temporal kinetics.

       Fourth, the full gene-level statistics from Wang et al. Supplementary Table 18 remain

incompletely extracted for the affected loci. This prevents a formal statement that each gene is

statistically nonsignificant in the V1-specific versus common EN-L4-IT comparison. The correct

record is that none is among the conspicuous published defining markers and exact per-gene

statistics remain pending archival verification.
                                                                                                182

       Finally, the available evidence does not justify attributing Irlen Syndrome, visual stress,

photophobia, pattern glare, cortical hyperexcitability, epilepsy, retinal degeneration, or another

clinical phenotype to this deletion. If later phases address such phenomena, they should be

decomposed into measurable traits and tested against molecular and physiological data rather

than treated as a single genetically established syndrome.

Phase 3 Final Conclusion

Phase 3 establishes that the 17p13.3 deletion intersects multiple genes with credible

       spatial and temporal opportunity in the human visual system. The strongest direct

anatomical convergence is RPE-centered and involves INPP5K, PITPNA, and SERPINF1.

Additional genes contribute broader retinal, neuronal, cortical, developmental, or cellular-

maintenance opportunity. Human dLGN data do not support preferential magnocellular

enrichment. Adult cortical data favor distributed rather than V1-specific expression.

Developmental multiomic and spatial atlases demonstrate that V1-specific programs are

detectable, yet the affected interval does not presently emerge as a major defining V1

developmental module.

       The resulting evidence supports moderate mechanistic plausibility for a distributed

reduction in visual-neural developmental and cellular functional reserve. It does not establish a

direct dosage-to-chemistry-to-circuit-to-phenotype chain. Phase 4 should therefore shift from

expression opportunity toward perturbational evidence: whether heterozygous reduction of the

highest-priority genes measurably changes phosphoinositide handling, membrane composition,

endosomal trafficking, trophic signaling, RNA processing, genome-stress responses,

neurite/circuit maturation, or combinations of these processes in relevant human cellular models.
                                                                                              183

Phase 3 Frozen Record

Phase 3 may be considered substantively complete subject to one archival refinement:

       retrieval of exact affected-gene rows from Wang et al. Supplementary Table 18. That

unresolved lookup should remain recorded as a quantitative limitation rather than converted into

a negative biological result. The principal conclusions are sufficiently replicated across

independent retinal, dLGN, adult-brain, developmental multiomic, and spatial-transcriptomic

resources to support transition to Phase 4.
                                                                                                  184

                                               Phase 4

    Dosage Sensitivity, Perturbation, Functional Convergence, and Integrated Adjudication

Abstract

       This frozen Phase 4 master report consolidates Phases 4A through 4G of the project

"Genomic and Molecular Analysis of a 17p13.3 Microdeletion and Its Candidate Visual-

Neurological Mechanisms." The structural reference remains GRCh37/hg19 chr17:1,411,408-

1,841,103, a nominal 429,696-bp heterozygous deletion affecting at least seventeen annotated

loci, including thirteen protein-coding genes. Phase 4 tested whether heterozygous dosage

reduction can move from genomic plausibility into experimentally observable molecular,

cellular, developmental, visual-system, and seizure-relevant consequences. The phase explicitly

separated four genetic states - heterozygous loss, biallelic or complete loss, hypomorphic

reduction, and specific pathogenic variants - and treated causality as the final rather than initial

inference.

       Across the complete audit, the strongest evidence supports a multi-layer

functionalreserve model rather than a single deleted "photosensitivity gene." RPA1 provides the

clearest direct human haploinsufficiency phenotype, SERPINF1 provides direct heterozygous

dosage reduction of PEDF, INPP5K provides one-copy physiological evidence outside the retina

and strong phosphoinositide/lysosomal mechanism, and several other genes produce relevant

phenotypes under severe or variant-specific perturbation. PRPF8, WDR81,

SERPINF1/PEDFassociated biology, and related systems converge on photoreceptor integrity;

PITPNA, WDR81, RILP, and INPP5K converge on membrane identity and intracellular material

handling; SERPINF1, RPA1, and SCARF1 define a layered protection-repair-clearance

architecture; and WDR81, INPP5K, RTN4RL1, and RILP converge on neuronal development
                                                                                                 185

and circuit organization. These convergences are biologically meaningful but do not constitute

demonstrated synergy or epistasis.

       The retinal evidence favors reduced resilience, impaired maintenance, delayed recovery,

and stress vulnerability substantially more strongly than increased photon-response gain. The

strongest partial-dose retinal physiology identified during Phase 4 shows reduced

electroretinographic output, not amplified output. Complete RTN4RL1 loss in human neurons

alters network construction but decreases synapse density and synchronous calcium-network

activity, arguing against a simple generalized-hyperexcitability interpretation. MIR22 provides

the strongest seizure-relevant locus-level evidence, but the decisive chronic epilepsy phenotype

was shown after complete loss and a prior epileptogenic insult, not after heterozygous loss or

photic stimulation.

Phase 4F localized the central causal gap. Human photosensitive epilepsy depends on

       frequency-sensitive visual-cortical responses, inhibitory containment, and abnormal

large-scale network propagation. The deletion has substantial biology at the retinal end and

meaningful general neuronal/white-matter biology downstream, but no current experiment

demonstrates abnormal lateral geniculate physiology, V1 flicker responses, visual-cortical

inhibition, 10-20-Hz entrainment, pathological occipital-to-distributed-network propagation, a

photoparoxysmal response, or visually triggered seizures produced by this exact CNV. Phase 4G

therefore classifies the deletion as a biologically consequential, mechanistically plausible

candidate modifier of visual-neural and epileptogenic reserve, not an established cause of

epileptic photosensitivity.
                                                                                                186

Phase 4 is frozen as Version 1.0 as of 15 September 2026. The frozen interpretation may

       be superseded only by explicitly reopened analysis or by new perturbational,

experimental, or clinical evidence. Phase 5 is reserved for perturbational validation against

independent in silico or existing experimental datasets.

Precedence

1. Structural and Evidentiary Precedence

       The structural coordinates used throughout Phase 4 are the original GRCh37/hg19

coordinates chr17:1,411,408-1,841,103. GRCh38 and current annotations may be used to

reconcile transcripts or expression resources, but they do not replace the primary CNV

definition. The interval includes thirteen protein-coding genes - INPP5K, PITPNA, SLC43A2,

SCARF1, RILP, PRPF8, TLCD2, WDR81, SERPINF2, SERPINF1, SMYD4, RPA1, and

RTN4RL1 - and four noncoding loci - PITPNA-AS1, MIR22HG, MIR22, and RN7SL105P.

INPP5K and RTN4RL1 are boundary genes. The reported-coordinate model removes substantial

INPP5K coding sequence and approximately 99 percent of the canonical RTN4RL1 coding

sequence on the affected allele, illustrating why genomic overlap percentage cannot be equated

with coding disruption percentage.

       The controlling evidence hierarchy for Phase 4 was genomic evidence -> molecular

       mechanism -> expression/spatiotemporal opportunity -> perturbational evidence ->

cellular phenotype -> developmental consequence -> visual-system physiology -> phenotype

association -> causality. This hierarchy was used because rare-CNV interpretation is especially

vulnerable to overreading knockout phenotypes, assuming that one-copy loss produces exactly

50 percent protein function, or transferring a specific dominant-negative or gain-of-function

disease mechanism to a simple deletion.
                                                                                                 187

2. Dosage-Sensitivity Precedence

        RPA1 provides the strongest direct precedent that a one-copy genomic loss inside this

broader chromosomal region can produce a measurable human molecular and cellular phenotype.

O'Driscoll and colleagues studied contiguous-gene deletion cell lines in which RPA1 was

haploinsufficient and found reduced RPA1 abundance, impaired ATR-dependent DNA-damage

signaling, defective checkpoint behavior after ultraviolet exposure, and increased

stressassociated nuclear fragmentation. Restoration of RPA1 rescued key abnormalities,

supporting a gene-specific haploinsufficiency effect rather than an unspecified contiguous-

deletion artifact (O'Driscoll et al.).

        SERPINF1 provides a second clinically relevant dosage precedent. Heterozygous carriers

of truncating SERPINF1 variants had reduced SERPINF1 transcript levels and significantly

lower serum PEDF concentrations without the severe skeletal phenotype of biallelic SERPINF1

disease (Al-Jallad et al.). This establishes that one functional copy can measurably lower the

gene product even when a classic Mendelian phenotype is absent.

        INPP5K heterozygous mouse data show that one-copy reduction can alter physiological

signaling outside the retina. Ijuin and colleagues found increased insulin sensitivity and altered

Akt-dependent glucose uptake in heterozygous SKIP/Inpp5k-deficient mice, demonstrating that a

one-copy state is not necessarily molecularly silent (Ijuin et al.). By contrast, WDR81 and

several other loci show dramatic phenotypes primarily under biallelic or severe loss, while

PITPNA displays substantial developmental buffering through the closely related paralog

PITPNB. These contrasting precedents required gene-specific rather than uniform dosage

assumptions.
                                                                                                188

       SERPINF2 serves as an important methodological positive control: heterozygous

alpha2antiplasmin deficiency can produce a clear biochemical dosage phenotype while

remaining mechanistically remote from retinal photon detection. Phase 4 therefore treated

"dosagesensitive" and "photosensitivity-relevant" as separate axes rather than synonyms.

3. Molecular and Chemical Precedence

3.1 TLCD2 and Membrane Composition

       Ruiz and colleagues showed that human TLCD1/2 influence membrane phospholipid

fatty-acyl composition and physical fluidity. TLCD2 knockdown altered incorporation of

longchain polyunsaturated fatty acids into phosphatidylcholine and phosphatidylethanolamine

and protected cells from palmitate-induced membrane rigidification under the tested conditions

(Ruiz et al.). The result establishes a real membrane-composition phenotype but also supplies

counterevidence against a simple assumption that TLCD2 loss is intrinsically harmful.

Petkevicius and colleagues later showed tissue- and paralog-dependent

phosphatidylethanolamine effects in Tlcd-family mouse models, emphasizing context and

redundancy.

3.2 SERPINF1/PEDF and PNPLA2/PEDF-R

       PEDF is a retinally important secreted protein encoded by SERPINF1. Subramanian and

colleagues identified functional PEDF-R/PNPLA2 interactions and linked PEDF signaling to

phospholipase/lipase activity and retinal cell survival (Subramanian et al.). Bernardo-Colon and

colleagues subsequently showed that Pnpla2 ablation causes photoreceptor degeneration, altered

retinal proteins and lipid species, apoptosis, and attenuated retinal electrophysiology;

heterozygous animals already demonstrated measurable physiological impairment (Bernardo-

Colon et al.). Rebustini and colleagues showed that Serpinf1 deletion produces senescence-like
                                                                                                189

RPE changes, reduced Pnpla2/PEDF-R expression, and accumulation of rhodopsin and lipids

after outer-segment phagocytosis (Rebustini et al.). Hara and colleagues further demonstrated

that PNPLA2 mobilizes retinyl esters from RPE retinosomes and that loss delays 11-cis-retinal

regeneration and dark adaptation (Hara et al.).

        The PEDF-DHA literature supplies an additional stress-protection precedent. Mukherjee,

Marcheselli, and Bazan demonstrated production of the DHA-derived mediator neuroprotectin

D1 in human RPE cells and showed protection against oxidative-stress-induced apoptosis

(Mukherjee et al.). This supports a chemically plausible SERPINF1/PEDF-linked retinal

stressdefense layer without establishing that heterozygous SERPINF1 loss alone reproduces the

complete downstream phenotype.

3.3 INPP5K

        McGrath and colleagues demonstrated that INPP5K hydrolyzes PI(4,5)P2 to PI4P on

autolysosomal membranes and is required for autophagic lysosome reformation. Strong loss

produces PI(4,5)P2 accumulation, PI4P depletion, persistent clathrin-associated reformation

tubules, enlarged autolysosomes, lysosome depletion, and impaired autophagic homeostasis;

wild-type INPP5K or reduction of PI(4,5)P2 synthesis can rescue key defects (McGrath et al.).

The evidence is mechanistically strong but is principally derived from skeletal-muscle systems

rather than retina.

3.4 WDR81

        Liu and colleagues identified WDR81/WDR91 as negative regulators of endosomal PI3P

and early-to-late endosome conversion. WDR81/WDR91 loss increases

Beclin1/VPS34associated activity, elevates PI3P-rich early-endosomal identity, enlarges

endosomes, and impairs receptor degradation (Liu et al.). This places WDR81 upstream in a
                                                                                                 190

membrane-identity and cargo-maturation system distinct from INPP5K's autolysosomal

PI(4,5)P2/PI4P function.

3.5 RILP

       RILP is a Rab7A effector and dynein-associated adaptor rather than an enzyme. Yap and

colleagues demonstrated that disruption of the RAB7A-RILP interaction decreases retrograde

late-endosome transport in dendrites, impairs dendritic cargo clearance, and reduces dendritic

arborization even when somatic lysosomal degradation remains comparatively preserved (Yap et

al.). This distinguishes physical cargo transport from terminal degradation and supports a

mechanochemical rather than catalytic interpretation of RILP.

3.6 PRPF8

       PRPF8 is a core U5 spliceosomal scaffold, not the catalytic metal-binding enzyme or

ATPase of the spliceosome. Human retinal-organoid models carrying pathogenic heterozygous

PRPF8 variants show that retina-specific transcript processing is unusually vulnerable to PRPF8

dysfunction. The H2309P model demonstrates altered splice-site selection, ciliary/polarity

abnormalities, and photoreceptor loss, while the Y2334N model preserves early retinal

specification but later develops markedly reduced photoreceptor inner/outer-segment structures

and retinal splicing abnormalities (Zimmann et al.; Atkinson et al.). These findings strongly

establish PRPF8 retinal relevance but do not demonstrate that whole-allele haploinsufficiency

behaves like the pathogenic mutant proteins.

3.7 RPA1 and Photochemical Stress

       The RPA1 precedent becomes relevant to light injury because retinal cells can experience

photochemically generated DNA damage. Sparrow, Zhou, and Cai showed that A2E-containing

RPE exposed to 430-nm illumination accumulates oxidative DNA lesions, including 8-oxo-dG,
                                                                                               191

and that a singlet-oxygen quencher reduces the damage (Sparrow et al.). Combining this

independent retinal-stress literature with direct RPA1 haploinsufficiency establishes a credible

but still untested retinal-reserve hypothesis: the same light-associated lesion burden could

become harder to manage when DNA-damage-response reserve is reduced.

3.8 Secondary Systems

       SCARF1 mediates C1q-associated recognition and efferocytosis of apoptotic cells;

SCARF1 deficiency impairs clearance and allows apoptotic and secondary-necrotic material to

accumulate (Ramirez-Ortiz et al.). SLC43A2/LAT4 is a facilitated-diffusion transporter for

neutral essential amino acids, with global or tissue-specific deletion producing strong systemic

transport phenotypes but limited direct retinal evidence (Guetg et al.; Rajendran et al.). SMYD4

is a SET/MYND-domain methyltransferase with experimentally demonstrated developmental

and chromatin effects, including H3K4-associated regulation and HDAC1 interaction in

zebrafish cardiac development, but no established visual-neural haploinsufficient phenotype

(Xiao et al.). These systems were therefore retained as secondary reserve mechanisms rather than

core photosensitivity drivers.

4. Cellular-Phenotype Precedence

       Several constituent-gene perturbations cross the threshold from molecular mechanism

into bona fide retinal or neuronal cellular phenotypes. Pathogenic PRPF8 variants produce

human retinal-organoid photoreceptor and outer-segment abnormalities. Severe WDR81

dysfunction causes progressive photoreceptor loss in mice, and wild-type WDR81 rescue

restores survival. The PEDF-R/PNPLA2 system produces photoreceptor deformation, apoptosis,

altered retinal proteins, and reduced ERG output. These independent systems therefore converge

on photoreceptor integrity through different upstream mechanisms.
                                                                                                192

       At the RPE level, complete Serpinf1 loss causes senescence-associated changes, impaired

outer-segment processing, and rhodopsin/lipid accumulation, while heterozygous Serpinf1 loss

already reduces downstream Pnpla2 expression. PNPLA2 loss further demonstrates delayed

retinoid mobilization and dark adaptation. The source literature thus supports genuine RPE

support-system dysfunction but does not establish that the complete heterozygous CNV itself has

been tested under visible-light challenge.

       For neural cells, RTN4RL1 is especially informative. Wang and colleagues showed that

complete loss in human neurons increases axonal and dendritic elaboration but decreases synapse

formation, multiple synaptic proteins, and synchronous calcium-network activity (Wang et al.).

This provides strong evidence that the gene can reorganize developing human neuronal networks

while simultaneously arguing against a simplistic hyperexcitability model.

5. Developmental Precedence

       WDR81 provides the strongest early corticogenesis precedent. Carpentieri and colleagues

showed that WDR81 loss alters endosomal EGFR trafficking, reduces MAPK signaling, slows

radial-glial proliferation during mid-to-late neurogenesis, and ultimately reduces brain size. A

Cyclin D2 manipulation rescues the proliferation phenotype, providing mechanistic continuity

from endosomal trafficking to cell-cycle control (Carpentieri et al.). The experiments principally

involve strong or complete loss, so transfer to heterozygous CNV dosage remains unresolved.

       PITPNA/PITPNB studies provide an explicit compensation precedent: severe combined

loss disrupts cortical progenitor polarity, Golgi phosphoinositide signaling, interkinetic nuclear

migration, and cortical development, whereas one remaining functional copy of either paralog

can support grossly normal brain development. This demonstrates that pathway essentiality does

not imply single-gene haploinsufficiency.
                                                                                                193

        RTN4RL1 and RILP occupy later developmental windows. RTN4RL1 loss alters neurite

growth and synaptogenesis in human neurons; RILP-dependent transport is required for normal

dendritic arborization. The two effects can even point in opposite morphological directions,

establishing topological convergence on circuit construction without directional convergence.

SERPINF1/PEDF contributes to postnatal retinal maturation and vascular/stress biology, while

PRPF8 pathogenic variants show preserved early retinal specification followed by later

photoreceptor maturation failure. MIR22 differs again: complete loss leaves baseline brain

structure comparatively normal but worsens chronic epilepsy after an epileptogenic insult,

placing its strongest effect in activity-dependent remodeling rather than constitutive brain

formation (Silva et al.).

6. Human CNV and White-Matter Precedence

        The most directly relevant clinical CNV precedent comes from interstitial 17p13.3

deletions located between YWHAE/CRK and PAFAH1B1. Emrick and colleagues reported

multifocal white-matter abnormalities in five of six individuals and narrowed a smallest

overlapping region to approximately 345 kb containing eleven RefSeq genes, with RTN4RL1

proposed as the strongest candidate (Emrick et al.). This evidence is substantially closer to the

present interval than classic 17p13.3 deletion syndromes involving PAFAH1B1 or

YWHAE/CRK. It supports neurological consequence and incomplete penetrance but does not

provide a replicated photoparoxysmal or visually triggered seizure phenotype.

7. Photosensitive-Epilepsy Precedence

        The disease physiology sets a high bar for any proposed genetic mechanism.

Photosensitive epilepsy is not simply excessive retinal photon detection. Timar and colleagues

found that PSE patients show increased occipital PPR power particularly during 10-20-Hz
                                                                                                  194

stimulation, while ordinary transient and steady-state visual-evoked responses are not uniformly

elevated. Propagating PPRs are associated especially with abnormal occipitofrontocentral

connectivity (Timar et al.). Benedetto and colleagues further reported greater visually evoked

EEG energy but reduced phase coherence and unstable alpha dynamics in photosensitive

idiopathic generalized epilepsy (Benedetto et al.). Earlier MEG work found gamma-band phase

clustering before transitions into PPRs (Parra et al.).

       Pattern-sensitive epilepsy further localizes the critical transformation to organized

cortical visual processing. Spatial frequency, orientation, contrast, and stimulus extent alter

epileptogenicity, and classic work argued against a trigger confined to the LGN (Soso, Lettich,

and Belgum; "Neurophysiological Aspects of Pattern-Sensitive Epilepsy"). TMS studies

demonstrate reduced normal inhibitory influence from visual to motor cortex in PPR-positive

patients, supporting a propagation/inhibitory-containment mechanism rather than a purely retinal

one (Strigaro et al.). Source-localization studies show occipital-to-parietal-to-frontal recruitment,

with thalamic participation becoming more prominent in some generalized events

("Representation and Propagation of Epileptic Activity").

       CHD2 supplies a useful genetic benchmark. Galizia and colleagues found rare CHD2

variants enriched in photosensitive epilepsy and showed that chd2 knockdown enhanced

photosensitivity in zebrafish (Galizia et al.). No locus within the present 17p13.3 interval

currently has comparable combined human-genetic and controlled-photic experimental evidence.
                                                                                              195

Our Findings

8. Phase 4A - Dosage Sensitivity and Photosensitivity-Centered Gene Audit

Phase 4A audited all thirteen affected protein-coding genes under a five-level evidence

        scale: A for direct heterozygous functional evidence; B for direct heterozygous molecular

or biochemical dosage evidence; C for strong perturbational evidence principally from severe,

biallelic, knockout, or otherwise non-equivalent models; D for mechanistic plausibility without

sufficiently relevant dosage perturbation; and E for insufficient meaningful evidence. Plus and

minus modifiers indicate position near a tier boundary. The grade measures dosage/perturbation

evidence, not photosensitivity relevance itself.

       The Phase 4A prioritization placed TLCD2, PRPF8, SERPINF1, INPP5K, and PITPNA

closest to the chemistry required to create, maintain, or regulate retinal light responses. RPA1,

WDR81, RILP, and RTN4RL1 were retained as major stress-, maintenance-, or neural-reserve

candidates. SCARF1, SLC43A2, SERPINF2, and SMYD4 remained lower-priority modifiers.

This ranking did not imply that the highest-priority genes were proven causal; it identified where

the shortest plausible path to the phenotype existed.

       The most important conceptual result of 4A was the separation of sensory

hypersensitivity from photochemical vulnerability. Sensory hypersensitivity requires the same

photons to produce a larger physiological or perceptual response. Photochemical vulnerability

requires the same photons to produce more molecular injury. RPA1 fits the second category far

better than the first. TLCD2 and PRPF8 were retained as more proximal candidates to
                                                                                             202

phototransduction or photoreceptor membrane behavior, while SERPINF1, INPP5K, PITPNA,

WDR81, and RILP primarily supported maintenance and reserve.

9. Phase 4B - Molecular Perturbation, Biochemical Flux, and Chemical Consequences

Phase 4B strengthened the evidentiary standard: gene function alone was no longer

       sufficient. Each mechanism was required to identify a perturbation, a measurable

molecular endpoint, direction, magnitude when available, cellular compartment, rescue evidence,

and proximity to the visual phenotype. This converted the project from an annotation exercise

into a perturbational chemistry audit.

9.1 TLCD2

       TLCD2 knockdown changes incorporation of selected long-chain polyunsaturated fatty

acids into PC and PE and changes membrane fluidity under palmitate challenge. The key

inference was not "TLCD2 loss makes membranes rigid." In the primary experiment, reduction

could protect against saturated-fat-induced rigidification. The correct Phase 4B conclusion was

that TLCD2 dosage can change membrane composition and therefore potentially alter the highly

specialized physical environment of photoreceptor outer-segment membranes, but the direction

in a DHA-rich human retina remains unpredictable.

9.2 SERPINF1/PEDF and PNPLA2

       The SERPINF1 arm produced one of the strongest molecular chains in the interval:

human heterozygous SERPINF1 loss lowers PEDF; PEDF stimulates

PEDF-R/PNPLA2associated phospholipase/lipase biology; PNPLA2 perturbation alters retinal

phospholipid species, outer-segment processing, retinal proteins, visual-cycle retinoid

mobilization, and ERG physiology. PEDF/DHA signaling additionally connects to

neuroprotectin D1 and oxidativestress protection. The project therefore retained SERPINF1 as a
                                                                                                 203

strong RPE/photoreceptorsupport candidate while explicitly refusing to equate Pnpla2

heterozygosity with Serpinf1 heterozygosity.

9.3 PITPNA and INPP5K

       PITPNA and INPP5K were found to occupy complementary but nonidentical

phosphoinositide roles. PITPNA supports local phosphatidylinositol/phosphatidylcholine transfer

and PI availability. INPP5K directly removes the 5-phosphate from PI(4,5)P2 and PIP3, with

strong-loss experiments demonstrating altered autolysosomal PI(4,5)P2/PI4P, persistent

clathrinassociated tubules, and impaired lysosome regeneration. Phase 4B emphasized that a

steady-state lipid concentration could appear normal even if turnover flux falls. Therefore a

combined PITPNA/INPP5K dosage defect could theoretically reduce phosphoinositide

regulatory reserve without producing a simple monotonic change in total PIP2.

9.4 WDR81 and RILP

       WDR81 and RILP form a sequential cellular-material-flow architecture rather than a

single biochemical pathway. WDR81 regulates early-endosomal PI3P and early-to-late

maturation; RILP couples Rab7-positive late endosomes to dynein-driven movement. The

critical distinction is that endosomal identity, physical transport, terminal degradation, and

lysosome recycling are separate control points. Phase 4B therefore described WDR81 -> RILP

-> INPP5K as functional adjacency, not as a proven molecular complex or epistatic chain.

9.5 PRPF8

       PRPF8 was treated as a production-side mechanism for photoreceptor machinery. Rather

than claiming that PRPF8 is itself phototransduction chemistry, Phase 4B positioned it upstream

of the transcript and protein products required for photoreceptor cilia, outer segments, and
                                                                                              204

signaling. Human retinal models of pathogenic PRPF8 variants strongly support retina-specific

splicing vulnerability; however, deletion-level haploinsufficiency remains unresolved.

9.6 RPA1

       RPA1 remained the strongest direct heterozygous molecular evidence in the entire

interval. Its Phase 4B role was narrowed to stress reserve: reduced RPA1 lowers capacity to

manage replication-associated and UV-associated damage in human cells. Independent RPE

studies show that blue-light photochemistry can generate oxidative DNA lesions. The project

therefore proposed a reserve model in which light-associated lesion production increases demand

while RPA1 haploinsufficiency lowers repair/checkpoint capacity. This bridge remains

inferential because no retinal RPA1+/- light-challenge experiment exists.

9.7 Lower-Priority Systems

       SCARF1 was retained as a post-injury clearance mechanism, not a canonical

photoreceptor-outer-segment receptor. SLC43A2 was retained for possible methionine/SAM,

glutathione, leucine/mTOR, and other metabolic reserve routes, but no retinal substrate limitation

was established. SMYD4 was retained as an epigenetic reserve candidate without retinal targets.

SERPINF2 remained a biochemical positive control with little direct photosensitivity relevance.

RTN4RL1 was restored to the Phase 4B analysis after an initial omission and was placed in a

central-neural branch involving neurite, synaptic, and network organization rather than retinal

chemistry.
