10. Phase 4C - Cellular Phenotype and Stress-Response Analysis

Phase 4C asked what the molecular abnormalities actually make retinal or neural cells do

       differently. Each claim was graded by genetic state, cell type, phenotype, baseline versus

stress dependence, rescue, and proximity to either sensory hypersensitivity or photochemical

vulnerability.

10.1 Photoreceptor Integrity

       The strongest cellular convergence was photoreceptor integrity. Pathogenic PRPF8

variants produce human retinal-organoid ciliary, outer-segment, and photoreceptor

abnormalities. Severe WDR81 dysfunction produces progressive photoreceptor death that is

rescued by wild-type WDR81. The SERPINF1-associated PEDF-R/PNPLA2 pathway produces

photoreceptor deformation, apoptosis, loss of retinal proteins, and measurable ERG impairment.

These mechanisms are independent, so their shared endpoint constitutes phenotypic convergence

rather than a single common upstream pathway.
                                                                                              209

       The direction of the physiology mattered. The strongest partial-dose retinal experiment

heterozygous Pnpla2 - showed decreased dark-adapted retinal output, not increased gain.

Complete PNPLA2 loss also delayed recovery after photobleaching. Therefore Phase 4C

materially weakened a simple "overresponsive retina" hypothesis.

       Quantitatively, heterozygous Pnpla2 mice had retinal thickness of approximately 177.9

+/- 2.156 micrometers at three months versus 209.2 +/- 9.12 micrometers in wild type (P <

.0075), and 175.5 +/- 5.31 micrometers at seven months versus 203.7 +/- 4.60 micrometers in

wild type (P < .007). At seven months, the dark-adapted b-wave averaged approximately 215.5

+/- 16.5 microvolts in heterozygotes versus 279.1 +/- 8.91 microvolts in wild type (P = .0023).

Photopic light-adapted ERG responses were not significantly different at the tested ages.

Heterozygous retinas also showed reduced rhodopsin and opsin abundance and substantially

reduced overlap between rod-bipolar and synaptic markers. These measurements reinforce that

partial PEDF-R/PNPLA2 pathway reduction can impair retinal structure and scotopic physiology

without demonstrating increased light-response gain (Bernardo-Colón et al.).

10.2 RPE Support and Outer-Segment Processing

       SERPINF1/PEDF became the experimentally strongest RPE arm. Serpinf1-null RPE

develops senescence-like changes and accumulates rhodopsin and lipids; one-copy loss

measurably reduces downstream Pnpla2 transcript abundance. PNPLA2 deficiency

independently impairs retinyl-ester mobilization and visual-cycle recovery. PRPF8 pathogenic

variants can also disturb RPE polarity and ciliary biology. By contrast, the Phase 3 RPE spatial

convergence of INPP5K + PITPNA + SERPINF1 was only partly validated at the cellular level:

direct heterozygous RPE phenotypes for INPP5K and PITPNA remain absent.
                                                                                               210

10.3 Endosomal/Lysosomal Cellular Phenotype

       WDR81 deficiency enlarges early endosomes and delays cargo progression; in the

neural-progenitor analysis, mean EEA1-positive early-endosome size increased by

approximately 63 percent under WDR81 deficiency. RILP-pathway disruption reduces dendritic

late-endosome movement and alters neuronal morphology. INPP5K loss prevents efficient

autolysosomal membrane recycling and lysosome replenishment. Together these data support a

serial material-handling architecture. They do not prove that heterozygous reduction of all three

genes in the same retinal cell produces an additive phenotype.

10.4 Stress Challenge

       The stress branch produced a coherent but incomplete protection-repair-clearance model.

PEDF/NPD1 biology can protect RPE from oxidative stress; RPA1 haploinsufficiency reduces

DNA-damage-response reserve; SCARF1 participates after cell death by clearing apoptotic

material. The critical full-CNV experiment - identical visible-light challenge in wild-type versus

heterozygous deletion retinal models - has not been performed.

10.5 Central Neural Phenotype

       RTN4RL1 became the strongest direct human-neuronal cellular candidate. Complete loss

increases neurite growth but decreases synaptic puncta by approximately 30-40 percent;

synchronous calcium peaks fall by approximately 40-80 percent and peak amplitudes by

approximately 50-70 percent across clones. RILP and WDR81 independently alter dendritic or

progenitor phenotypes. MIR22 complete loss worsens later spontaneous epilepsy after status

epilepticus: knockout mice averaged roughly 16 seizures per day versus 2.47 in wild type, with

mean seizure duration approximately 18.4 seconds versus 11.9 seconds. Chronic heterozygous

animals were not followed in the decisive seizure phase, and no photic stimulation was used. The
                                                                                           211

combined interpretation was altered neural reserve and network construction, not demonstrated

hyperexcitability (Wang et al.; Silva et al.).

11. Phase 4D - Developmental Perturbation and Critical Windows

Phase 4D tested whether affected genes change how retinal and neural systems are built,

       rather than only how mature cells function. The key result was distributed timing. No

single developmental "hit" explained the interval. Instead, different loci act at progenitor,

differentiation, neurite, synaptic, retinal-maturation, and post-insult remodeling stages.

       WDR81 was the strongest early corticogenesis candidate. Severe loss reduces mid-to-late

radial-glial proliferation and later-born cortical-neuron production/positioning through altered

endosomal growth-factor signaling. PITPNA-associated developmental biology operates in a

similar broad window but is strongly buffered by PITPNB, preventing transfer of the severe

combined-loss phenotype to simple PITPNA heterozygosity.

       INPP5K reduction affects neuronal process elaboration and strong-loss ocular

development. RTN4RL1 and RILP act later on axon/dendrite and synapse construction, with

partly opposing morphological directions. SERPINF1/PEDF contributes to postnatal retinal

maturation, vascular development, trophic support, and stress resilience. PRPF8 pathogenic

organoids show relatively intact early neural-retina specification followed by late photoreceptor

maturation failure. MIR22 has little evidence for gross constitutive brain malformation; its

strongest role appears during epileptogenic remodeling after injury.
                                                                                                 215

        The developmental phase also strengthened the concept of compensation.

PITPNA/PITPNB redundancy, PEDF developmental catch-up in some vascular measures,

preserved early PRPF8 retinal specification, and normal baseline MIR22-null anatomy all

demonstrate that normal baseline structure does not imply normal reserve. Phase 4D therefore

favored a distributed critical-window vulnerability model over a congenital V1-specific or

magnocellular lesion.

        The noncoding loci were also constrained explicitly. MIR22 is embedded within

MIR22HG, so MIR22HG and MIR22 cannot be counted as two independent deletion events

supporting the same mechanism. MIR22HG has no independently validated visualdevelopmental

mechanism sufficient to add separate weight. PITPNA-AS1 remains dominated by context-

dependent cancer-cell literature rather than normal retinal or neural dosage biology. RN7SL105P

has no validated locus-specific function that warrants mechanistic weight. These negative

assignments are retained in the frozen report to prevent artificial inflation of the multigene

convergence model.

12. Phase 4E - Multi-Gene Functional Convergence

Phase 4E tested whether the independently supported effects actually meet. Five levels of

       convergence were distinguished: spatial co-localization, molecular-system convergence,

sequential-process convergence, phenotypic convergence, and direct combinatorial convergence.

The first four were observed to varying degrees; the fifth was not. No relevant primary study

jointly reduced two loci from this exact interval and measured a retinal or photosensitivityrelated

outcome. Accordingly, Phase 4E used "functional convergence" and explicitly withheld

"synergy" and "epistasis."

12.1 Membrane Identity and Material Flow

       PITPNA, WDR81, and INPP5K converge on phosphoinositide-dependent membrane

identity but regulate different lipid pools in different compartments. PITPNA supports PI/PC

transfer and PI4P-generating signaling, WDR81 restrains VPS34-dependent PI3P on endosomes,

and INPP5K turns over PI(4,5)P2 on autolysosomal membranes. RILP then links late-endosomal

identity to physical transport. This architecture is distributed, not a linear PI -> PI3P -> PIP2

cascade.

           The most coherent serial cellular architecture was WDR81 -> RILP -> INPP5K,

       representing endosome maturation -> late-endosome transport ->

autolysosomal/lysosomal recycling. A serial system can be limited by its weakest stage, so multi-

gene effects need not be additive. One defect can mask another, multiple partial defects can

remain silent until cargo demand rises, or the system can display threshold behavior.

12.2 Photoreceptor Endpoint Convergence

       PRPF8, WDR81, and the SERPINF1/PEDF-R system independently converge on

photoreceptor structural and functional integrity through RNA processing, intracellular
                                                                                                220

maintenance, and trophic/lipid support. TLCD2 adds a plausible membrane-composition route

without direct retinal cellular validation. This was the strongest phenotypic convergence

identified in Phase 4.

12.3 Stress-Defense Convergence

       SERPINF1, RPA1, and SCARF1 map onto different layers of stress response: damage

tolerance/protection, DNA-damage response/repair reserve, and post-injury clearance. The

architecture is coherent because all three affect how tissue withstands or resolves injury, but they

have not been jointly tested in one retinal model.

12.4 Circuit-Construction Convergence

       WDR81, INPP5K, RTN4RL1, and RILP converge topologically on neural development

and network construction. Directional coherence is weaker. RTN4RL1 knockout increases

neurite branching while RILP-pathway disruption reduces dendritic arborization; PITPNA is

buffered by PITPNB; and RTN4RL1 knockout decreases synchronous activity. Phase 4E

therefore concluded that the interval perturbs multiple controls of network topology without

demonstrating a unified hyperexcitable direction.

13. Phase 4F - Visual-System Relevance and Signal Propagation

Phase 4F mapped the evidence node by node from photoreceptor to photoparoxysmal

       response. The major result was asymmetry: the deletion has its strongest biological

evidence before the optic nerve and in general network-development systems, while the most

phenotypespecific stages - V1 flicker physiology and distributed PPR propagation - remain

untested.

13.1 Photoreceptor and RPE Nodes

       PRPF8, SERPINF1/PEDF, WDR81, TLCD2, PITPNA, and INPP5K provide meaningful

retinal mechanisms. The evidence overwhelmingly concerns structural integrity, adaptation,

trafficking, lipid support, or stress tolerance rather than increased photon gain. The strongest

partial-dose physiology shows reduced ERG response; PNPLA2 loss slows dark adaptation.

Therefore primary retinal amplification was downgraded to weak/unsupported, while altered

retinal temporal coding remained plausible but untested.

13.2 RGC, Optic Nerve, and White Matter

       PEDF directly supports RGC survival in injury models. RTN4RL1 is expressed in retinal

ganglion cells and participates in growth-inhibitory/plasticity systems, but Nogo-receptor family
                                                                                                  224

redundancy is substantial: even combined family loss can leave baseline retinal stratification,

optic-nerve myelination, and central RGC projections grossly normal. This weakens a simple

optic-pathway miswiring hypothesis.

       Human overlapping-CNV evidence nevertheless introduces a credible white-matter

branch. The Emrick series demonstrates multifocal leukoencephalopathy in small interstitial

17p13.3 deletions excluding YWHAE and PAFAH1B1, with RTN4RL1 proposed as a candidate.

White-matter abnormalities could affect conduction timing and long-range synchronization, but

the reported lesions were not shown to selectively involve optic radiations or occipitofrontal

pathways and no photic EEG phenotype was reported.

13.3 LGN and V1

       Earlier expression analysis found no affected interval gene among the principal defining

markers of human magnocellular, parvocellular, or koniocellular dLGN populations. Phase 4

therefore did not support a selective magnocellular mechanism. No deletion-specific LGN

hyperexcitability, temporal gain, or thalamocortical entrainment phenotype has been

demonstrated.

       V1 is the central evidence gap. Human PSE physiology strongly implicates occipital

cortex, contrast/gain control, temporal entrainment, inhibition, and network connectivity. The

deletion contains cortical and developmental genes, but none has been shown in the

heterozygous CNV state to produce abnormal V1 responses to flicker. There is no direct

evidence of altered P100, SSVEP, gamma synchronization, alpha stabilization, or V1 inhibitory

containment attributable to this CNV.
                                                                                             225

13.4 Distributed Propagation and Seizure Threshold

       PSE often involves occipital-to-parietal-to-frontal spread and abnormal visuomotor

connectivity. RTN4RL1, RILP, WDR81, INPP5K, and white-matter findings make altered

network topology biologically plausible. Yet no affected-locus or CNV model has demonstrated

pathological propagation during visual stimulation. MIR22 is best positioned downstream as a

possible modifier of epileptogenic threshold or remodeling rather than a light-trigger mechanism.

14. Phase 4G - Integrated Adjudication

Phase 4G formally separated biological consequence from phenotype attribution. The

       CNV is very likely biologically non-neutral: it includes directly dosage-responsive genes,

multiple retinal pathways, strong severe-loss or pathogenic-variant mechanisms,

neuraldevelopment genes, and a clinically relevant overlapping-deletion white-matter phenotype.

However, biological consequence is not equivalent to epileptic-photosensitivity causation.

       Broad 17p13.3 epilepsy literature cannot be transferred wholesale because many

published deletion syndromes include PAFAH1B1 and/or YWHAE/CRK, which are outside this

interval. The closest human CNV precedent supports white-matter vulnerability rather than a
                                                                                                 229

defined photosensitive-epilepsy syndrome. Likewise, no locus in this interval meets the CHD2

benchmark of human photosensitivity association plus controlled photic experimental validation.

        The integrated causal ladder therefore stops before the disease-specific physiological

steps. Genomic lesion and dosage alteration are established. Molecular consequences are

established for selected genes. Relevant cellular phenotypes are established in constituent-gene

models. Multi-gene functional convergence is supported. A full-CNV retinal phenotype is not

established. Abnormal visually evoked cortical physiology is not established. A PPR and a

photosensitive seizure caused by the exact CNV are not established.

15. Frozen Phase 4 Mechanistic Model

       The strongest integrated model after 4A-4G is a multi-layer visual-neural

functionalreserve lesion. It does not require every deleted gene to be individually

haploinsufficient, nor does it require every perturbation to move in the same direction. Instead,

the interval can lower or alter reserve at several control layers while paralogs, developmental

compensation, homeostatic adaptation, and opposite-direction effects preserve baseline function.

17p13.3 deletion -> retinal maintenance reserve down + membrane/endosomal reserve down +

       stress-response reserve down + developmental/network organization altered

       Under this model, baseline demand can remain below the reduced reserve of the

heterozygous system, allowing apparently normal anatomy or physiology. Increased demand

intense or repetitive visual stimulation, oxidative stress, developmental maturation, metabolic

stress, illness, or a separate epileptogenic influence - may expose deficits that are otherwise

compensated.

       If the CNV ultimately contributes to epileptic photosensitivity, Phase 4 indicates that the

most plausible route is not excessive photon capture. The stronger hypothesis is altered retinal

timing/adaptation and/or altered circuit construction or white-matter/network organization,

combined with reduced epileptogenic reserve, producing a lower threshold for abnormal
                                                                                                 231

propagation of patterned visual activity. This remains a testable hypothesis rather than a

demonstrated mechanism.

16. Hypotheses Retained, Downgraded, or Rejected

16.1 Retained as Best-Supported

   The deletion is biologically consequential and can reduce molecular/cellular reserve.

   Retinal and RPE maintenance pathways are genuinely represented in the interval.

   Photoreceptor structural integrity is the strongest phenotype-level convergence.

   Endosomal/lysosomal material handling is the strongest serial mechanistic convergence.

   Distributed developmental and network-organization effects are plausible and

       experimentally supported under strong perturbation.

   The deletion is a plausible candidate modifier of visual-neural and epileptogenic reserve.

16.2 Retained but Unproven

   Increased retinal vulnerability to the same light dose.

   Abnormal retinal temporal coding or adaptation at provocative flicker frequencies.

   Altered long-range visual-network propagation because of circuit-development or white-

       matter changes.

   A modest lowering of epileptogenic threshold in the heterozygous CNV state.

16.3 Downgraded or Unsupported

   A simple intrinsically hyperresponsive retina.

   A selective magnocellular lesion.

   A deletion-wide V1-specific developmental program.

   Generalized RTN4RL1-driven hyperexcitability.

   Uniformly harmful TLCD2-mediated membrane rigidification.
                                                                                               232

   Proven synergy or epistasis among deleted genes.

   Established direct causation of PPR or photosensitive seizures by this exact CNV.

17. Definitive Experiments Implied by Phase 4

       The single highest-value experimental strategy is an isogenic reconstruction of the actual

heterozygous deletion rather than a collection of unrelated null models. Wild-type, full-CNV

heterozygous, corrected/rescued, and selected single-gene heterozygous lines should be

differentiated into retinal organoids, RPE, and connected neural systems. The engineered interval

should reproduce the reported hg19 coordinates as closely as technically possible, particularly

the boundary structures of INPP5K and RTN4RL1.

       The retinal arm should measure photoreceptor structure, outer-segment formation,

retinal/RPE lipidomics, phosphoinositide pools, outer-segment phagocytosis and degradation,

visual-cycle retinoids, dark adaptation, ERG-like responses, oxidative/DNA damage, and

recovery after calibrated light exposure. The neural arm should measure progenitor behavior,

neurite morphology, synapse density, E/I balance, calcium dynamics, MEA firing and bursting,

frequency entrainment, phase locking, and long-range propagation.

       For photosensitivity specifically, patterned visual stimulation must be treated as a

transfer-function problem. Identical stimuli should be delivered across relevant flicker

frequencies, contrasts, wavelengths, and spatial structures while measuring the transformation

from retinal output through thalamic and V1-like networks into distributed activity. Only

demonstration of genotype-dependent pathological propagation, followed by correction or

rescue, would close the major causal gap identified in Phase 4.
                                                                                                   233

Summary

Phase 4 converts the 17p13.3 interval from a list of deleted genes into a disciplined

       mechanistic model. The interval contains several genes with real dosage or perturbational

consequences, and those consequences repeatedly touch retinal support, photoreceptor integrity,

membrane identity, intracellular trafficking, DNA-damage response, neuronal development,

synaptic organization, and network plasticity. The biological signal is therefore nonrandom and

medically relevant.

       At the same time, the phase narrows rather than broadens the photosensitivity claim. The

data do not show that the retina is intrinsically overresponsive to individual photons. The best

retinal physiology instead shows reduced response or delayed recovery. The best centralneuronal

RTN4RL1 model shows network remodeling with reduced synchronous activity. The strongest

seizure-related locus, MIR22, modifies post-insult epileptogenesis under complete loss but has

not been shown to create a photic phenotype in heterozygotes.

       The strongest surviving interpretation is reduced or altered functional reserve across

multiple layers. Photoreceptor/RPE systems may be less resilient; endosomal and lysosomal

material handling may have less capacity; damage-response reserve may be reduced; and circuit

construction or long-range communication may be subtly altered. Compensation and redundancy

can keep baseline function apparently normal. The model predicts that differences may emerge

under increased demand or repeated challenge rather than as an obligatory congenital deficit.

       For epileptic photosensitivity, the unresolved stage is now precisely located. Human

disease physiology requires the transformation of patterned visual input into abnormal occipital

and distributed cortical activity. The present CNV has not been shown to alter V1 flicker

physiology, cortical inhibition, 10-20-Hz entrainment, long-range visual-network propagation, a
                                                                                                  234

photoparoxysmal response, or visually triggered seizures. Therefore the exact CNV cannot

currently be classified as an established cause of epileptic photosensitivity.

       The frozen Phase 4 classification is: biologically consequential CNV; high retinal/visual

mechanistic relevance; moderate-to-high support for reduced functional reserve; low support for

direct sensory amplification; moderate support for developmental/network modification;

limitedto-moderate evidence for general epileptogenic modification; insufficient evidence for

abnormal visually driven cortical propagation; and insufficient evidence for direct

epilepticphotosensitivity causation. The best current label is "mechanistically plausible candidate

modifier of visual-neural and epileptogenic reserve."

Vocabulary

Adaptation. Adjustment of retinal or neural responsiveness during or after

sustained/repeated

       stimulation; dark adaptation specifically refers to recovery of visual sensitivity after

photobleaching.

Autolysosomal reformation (ALR). Membrane-recycling process that regenerates

lysosomes

       from autolysosomes after prolonged autophagy; INPP5K is required for normal ALR.

Biallelic loss. Disruption of both gene copies. Biallelic loss is not equivalent to the

heterozygous

       one-copy loss produced by this CNV.

Candidate modifier. A factor plausibly capable of changing severity, threshold, penetrance,

or

       expression of a phenotype without being established as a sufficient primary cause.
                                                                                                 235

Cellular phenotype. A measurable change in cell structure, survival, trafficking, signaling,

       electrophysiology, or stress response following perturbation.

CNV. Copy-number variant; a genomic deletion or duplication altering the number of

copies of

       one or more loci.

Directional convergence. Multiple perturbations pushing a shared endpoint in the same

       direction. This is distinct from merely affecting the same system.

Dosage sensitivity. The degree to which changing gene copy number alters transcript,

protein,

       molecular function, cell behavior, or clinical phenotype.

Efferocytosis. Recognition and engulfment of apoptotic cells or cellular debris by

phagocytic

       cells; SCARF1 participates in C1q-associated efferocytosis.

Electroretinography (ERG). Measurement of retinal electrical responses to light. The a-

wave

       primarily reflects photoreceptor activity; the b-wave reflects downstream inner-retinal

responses.

Endolysosomal system. The linked endosome-to-lysosome network that sorts, transports,

       degrades, and recycles cellular cargo.
                                                                                                   236

Epileptogenic reserve. Conceptual resistance of a neural network to transitions into

persistent or

        recurrent epileptic activity; lower reserve implies easier pathological transition under

challenge. Epistasis. A genetic interaction in which the effect of one locus depends on the state

of another.

Phase 4 did not demonstrate epistasis among deleted loci.

Functional convergence. Independent genes or mechanisms affecting the same molecular

        system, cellular process, anatomical compartment, developmental operation, or

phenotype endpoint.

Functional reserve. Difference between a system's maximal or available biological capacity

and

        the physiological demand placed on that system.

Gain. Ratio between change in output and change in input. Increased sensory gain means a

        larger physiological response to the same stimulus.

Haploinsufficiency. A condition in which one functional gene copy is insufficient for normal

        molecular, cellular, or clinical function.

Heterozygous loss. Loss or disruption of one of two gene copies. It does not automatically

imply

        exactly 50 percent protein abundance or function. Hypomorph. An allele that retains

partial gene function rather than producing complete loss.

Isogenic. Genetically matched experimental lines differing only at the engineered locus or

CNV

        of interest, minimizing background-genome confounding.
                                                                                                 237

Koniocellular, magnocellular, parvocellular. Major subdivisions of primate visual-thalamic

       organization. Phase 4 did not identify selective magnocellular involvement by the

deletion.

Lateral geniculate nucleus (LGN). Principal visual relay nucleus of the thalamus between

       retinal ganglion cells and primary visual cortex.

MIR22. MicroRNA locus within the deletion. Complete loss worsens chronic epilepsy after

an

       epileptogenic insult in mice; heterozygous photic effects remain unknown.

Neuroprotectin D1 (NPD1). DHA-derived lipid mediator with anti-inflammatory and anti-

       apoptotic effects in RPE under oxidative stress.

Outer segment. Specialized photoreceptor compartment containing stacked photopigment-

rich

       membranes where photon absorption and phototransduction begin.

PEDF. Pigment epithelium-derived factor, encoded by SERPINF1; a secreted retinal

trophic,

       antiangiogenic, lipid-linked, and cytoprotective protein.

Photochemical vulnerability. Greater molecular or cellular injury from the same photon

       exposure, distinct from sensory hypersensitivity.

Photoparoxysmal response (PPR). Abnormal epileptiform EEG response evoked by

       intermittent photic or pattern stimulation; it can occur with or without a clinical seizure.

Photosensitive epilepsy (PSE). Epilepsy in which visual stimulation can provoke abnormal

       EEG activity and, in some patients, clinical seizures.
                                                                                               238

Phototransduction. Conversion of absorbed photons into an electrical response in

       photoreceptors through rhodopsin/opsin, transducin, PDE6, cGMP, and cyclic-

nucleotidegated channel signaling.

Phosphoinositide. Phosphorylated phosphatidylinositol lipid that helps define membrane

       identity, trafficking, signaling, and protein recruitment.

PI3P. Phosphatidylinositol 3-phosphate, enriched on early endosomes and produced by

VPS34;

       WDR81/WDR91 negatively regulate this system.

PI4P. Phosphatidylinositol 4-phosphate, a membrane signaling lipid and product of

INPP5K-

       mediated PI(4,5)P2 dephosphorylation.

PI(4,5)P2. Phosphatidylinositol 4,5-bisphosphate, a membrane signaling lipid whose 5-

       phosphate is removed by INPP5K.

Retinal ganglion cell (RGC). Output neuron of the retina whose axons form the optic nerve

and

       project to visual brain targets.

Retinal pigment epithelium (RPE). Support epithelium adjacent to photoreceptor outer

       segments; essential for phagocytosis, retinoid cycling, lipid handling, trophic support, and

metabolic homeostasis.

Sensory hypersensitivity. An abnormally large perceptual or physiological response to the

same

       light stimulus.
                                                                                                 239

Spliceosome. Large ribonucleoprotein machinery that removes introns from pre-mRNA;

PRPF8

        is a central U5 spliceosomal scaffold.

Steady-state visual evoked potential (SSVEP). Stimulus-frequency-locked cortical response

        produced by periodic visual stimulation; useful for evaluating visual entrainment and

temporal stability.

Synergy. A combined effect greater than expected under a specified additive model. Phase

4 did

        not demonstrate synergy among deleted genes.

Temporal functional convergence. Different loci influencing the same biological endpoint at

        different developmental or physiological stages rather than acting simultaneously.

Topological convergence. Different perturbations affecting the same biological network or

        structural endpoint even if they push that endpoint in different directions.

V1. Primary visual cortex, the principal cortical recipient of LGN visual input and a major

        initiation node for photoparoxysmal responses.

Visual-neural reserve. Integrated capacity of retinal, visual-pathway, and cortical systems

to

        maintain stable function under physiological or pathological demand.

Quantitative definitions can be applied directly to future datasets. Conceptual equations are

explicitly labeled and should not be mistaken for validated physiological laws.

                                              Phase 5

 Deterministic Evidence Integration and Reproducibility Analysis - Available Frozen Summary

                                                v1.0

Phase 5 - Casual Reader Summary

        What the genome research found, without the technical detail. FROZEN SCIENTIFIC

RECORD - VERSION 1.0. Prepared by Zachariah P. Laing, 29 September 2026.

This is a plain-language companion to the frozen Phase 5 scientific record. It simplifies

terminology but does not change the scientific conclusion.

The short version

        The deletion does not look biologically meaningless. It removes or disrupts a group of

genes that have real roles in the retina, brain development, nerve-cell communication, membrane

biology, cellular cleanup, and stress responses. The evidence does not point cleanly to one single

gene as the explanation, and it also does not support treating every deleted gene as an equal 50%

loss.

        The best overall explanation is that the deletion may reduce biological "reserve" across

several systems at once. In this model, many individual effects can be mild, partly compensated,

or only become important under stress. That fits the available research better than a single-gene

explanation or a simple sum of seventeen harmful effects.

        The original question involved photosensitivity and visually triggered seizures. Phase 5

does not prove that connection. Instead, it shows that broader eye and brain phenotypes are

currently better supported than photosensitivity itself.
                                                                                                246

What appears most worth paying attention to

1. Retinal/RPE functional reserve - This is the strongest eye-related signal. Several deleted

   genes affect photoreceptors, retinal support cells, cilia, phagocytosis, or retinal maintenance.

2. Brain development and neural-network organization - Several genes affect neurites,

   synapses, neuronal survival, trafficking, or coordinated network activity.

3. Stress-dependent vulnerability - A recurring pattern is that cells may look relatively normal

   at baseline but become less resilient under injury, oxidative stress, heavy activity, aging, or

   other challenge.

4. Seizure susceptibility / epileptogenesis - There is some direct epilepsy-related evidence,

   especially around MIR22, but it does not show that this exact one-copy deletion causes

   epilepsy.

5. Retinal timing / visual signal processing - Plausible, but not directly measured in the exact

   CNV.

6. Cortical synchronization / flicker entrainment - Mechanistically relevant, but currently a

   missing link rather than a demonstrated phenotype.

7. Photosensitivity / photoparoxysmal response - Still plausible, but it sits furthest downstream

   and currently has the least direct evidence of the major candidate outcomes.

Why one gene is probably not the whole story

       Some genes in the deletion have very strong eye or brain effects when they are

completely knocked out or heavily damaged. The problem is that the actual CNV is

heterozygous: one copy is affected and one copy remains. For several genes, one copy is enough

to avoid the severe phenotype seen in full knockouts.
                                                                                                 247

       PRPF8 is a good example: reduced RNA does not necessarily mean reduced protein.

PITPNA and WDR81 show threshold or recessive behavior, where the severe phenotype appears

mainly after much greater loss of function. That makes it risky to claim that any one of these

genes automatically explains the whole CNV phenotype.

Why "all the genes add up" is also too simple

       Not every deleted gene pushes biology in the same direction. In some experiments, losing

TLCD2, MIR22HG, or SERPINF2 can actually be protective under specific stress conditions.

RTN4RL1 loss can reduce coordinated neural activity rather than increase it. These findings do

not make the genes irrelevant; they show that their effects depend on context.

       That is why the leading model is a distributed reserve model rather than a simple

arithmetic total of gene losses.

What the simulations actually contributed

       The simulations were not used to invent biological evidence. They were used to answer

methodological questions: how many biological replicates would be needed, how easily small

effects could be missed, how often frequency scans generate false positives, and whether a

distributed mechanism could be mistaken for a single-gene mechanism.

   The final standardized simulation suite used 20,000 replicates per scenario/cell where

    applicable, totaling about 1.88 million primary stochastic draws/resamples.

   With that many runs, Monte Carlo sampling error became very small; the larger uncertainty

    now comes from biology, not from the number of simulations.

   Small one-copy effects can be difficult to detect with too few independent cell lines or

    clones.

   Using many cells from the same clone does not replace independent biological replication.
                                                                                                248

   Scanning many flicker frequencies without correction can produce apparently significant

    results by chance surprisingly often.

   Small datasets can make a distributed mechanism look like a simpler single-gene

    explanation.

So what does Phase 5 actually conclude?

       The deletion is best viewed as a candidate distributed visual-neural reserve lesion, not as

a proven single-gene photosensitivity mutation.

       The strongest candidate consequences are subtle retinal/RPE dysfunction, altered neural

development or network organization, and reduced resilience under stress. There is also

moderate support for an effect on seizure susceptibility or epileptogenesis. Photosensitivity

remains possible, but the key physiological chain from the retina through the thalamus and visual

cortex to a photoparoxysmal response has not been directly measured for this exact CNV.

What has NOT been proven

   That every deleted gene loses exactly half of its function.

   That the genes act synergistically.

   That the exact CNV causes a measurable retinal abnormality.

   That the CNV causes abnormal visual-cortex excitation or flicker entrainment.

   That the CNV causes a photoparoxysmal response or visually triggered seizure.

   That one specific gene is the sole driver.

Why the result is still useful

       The project started with a broad question about photosensitivity. Phase 5 narrowed the

problem. It now appears more defensible to study the CNV as a broader visual-neural reserve

problem, with retinal maintenance, neural-network organization, and stress response as the
                                                                                               249

strongest candidate phenotype domains. That is more specific than simply saying "we do not

know," while still respecting the fact that the missing physiological measurements have not been

made.

        The next project stage, Phase 6 - Conclusion, integrates this frozen Phase 5 record with

the earlier genomic, mechanistic, and expression phases. Phase 6 is a separate master conclusion

and does not alter the frozen Phase 5 findings.
                                                                                                     250

                                                Phase 6

        Project Conclusion, Critical Appraisal, Forward Research Paths, and Frozen Record

Fixed structural reference: GRCh37/hg19 chr17:1,411,408-1,841,103 | heterozygous deletion | nominal span

429,696 bp | 17 affected annotated loci | INPP5K and RTN4RL1 boundary-intersected


1. Purpose and Phase 6 Status

         Phase 6 concludes the first-pass in silico research program. It does not reopen or silently

revise the frozen findings of Phases 1-5. Its role is to consolidate what the project established,

identify what remains unsupported or unresolved, critically assess the methodology and

inferential limits, define two legitimate continuation paths, and establish a version-controlled

stopping point from which future work can proceed.

         The project began with a broad visual-neurological question, including possible

photosensitivity and visually triggered seizure mechanisms. The accumulated evidence narrowed

that question. The strongest defensible interpretation is now a distributed visual-neural

functional-reserve model involving retinal/RPE support, membrane and phosphoinositide

biology, endosomal/lysosomal trafficking, genome-stress response, neural development, and

network organization. The exact heterozygous CNV has not been shown to cause abnormal

visual-cortical flicker processing, a photoparoxysmal response, or visually triggered seizures.

2. Project Facts Preserved at Freeze

         Structural reference remains GRCh37/hg19 chr17:1,411,408-1,841,103, nominally

429,696 bp, heterozygous.

         The interval affects at least 17 annotated loci: 13 protein-coding loci and 4 noncoding

loci.
                                                                                                 251

       Eleven protein-coding genes are fully encompassed: PITPNA, SLC43A2, SCARF1,

RILP, PRPF8, TLCD2, WDR81, SERPINF2, SERPINF1, SMYD4, and RPA1.

       INPP5K and RTN4RL1 are boundary-intersected. Under the reported-coordinate model,

approximately 49.6% of INPP5K MANE amino-acid-coding sequence and approximately 99%

of RTN4RL1 CCDS sequence on the affected allele lie within the deletion.

       The noncoding loci PITPNA-AS1, MIR22HG, MIR22, and RN7SL105P are included in

the frozen interval.

       The reported array coordinates are operational CNV boundaries, not demonstrated

nucleotide-exact chromosome junctions.

3. Integrated Findings of the First-Pass Research

3.1 Structural genomics

       Phase 1 converted a generic 17p13.3 label into a reproducible locus-specific substrate.

The main structural result was that apparent genomic-span overlap can substantially understate

coding disruption at the two boundary genes. The project therefore correctly anchored all later

interpretation to the exact reported interval rather than importing phenotypes from broader

17p13.3 deletion syndromes.

3.2 Molecular and biochemical architecture

       Phase 2 showed that the interval is not biologically featureless. Multiple affected genes

occupy mechanistically relevant systems: phosphoinositide metabolism, phospholipid transfer

and membrane composition, late-endosome and lysosome biology, intracellular transport, retinal

trophic support, RNA splicing, DNA replication and repair, neuronal structural signaling, amino-

acid transport, immune clearance, and fibrinolysis. The strongest mechanistic convergence

involved INPP5K, PITPNA, TLCD2, WDR81, RILP, and SERPINF1, with RTN4RL1 adding
                                                                                                 252

neuronal surface and structural signaling. This was evidence of functional adjacency and

convergence, not proof of multigene synergy or epistasis.

3.3 Spatial and developmental opportunity

       Phase 3 established where affected mechanisms could plausibly operate. The clearest

direct cell-localized convergence was RPE-centered, particularly INPP5K, PITPNA, and

SERPINF1. PRPF8 showed broad retinal relevance, and other loci provided distributed retinal,

neuronal, cortical, and developmental opportunity. The negative results were equally

informative: the affected interval did not emerge as a preferential magnocellular program in

human dLGN data, and adult/developmental cortical analyses did not support a simple V1-

specific expression model. Expression was explicitly treated as opportunity rather than

dysfunction.

3.4 Dosage, perturbation, and phenotype-level evidence

       Phase 4 moved beyond expression to perturbational evidence and genotype-state

discipline. RPA1 provided the clearest direct human haploinsufficiency precedent; SERPINF1

showed direct heterozygous molecular dosage reduction of PEDF; INPP5K provided one-copy

physiological evidence outside the retina together with strong phosphoinositide/lysosomal

mechanism. Other loci supplied strong but less genotype-equivalent evidence from biallelic loss,

knockout, hypomorphs, or variant-specific disease mechanisms. The project repeatedly preserved

the distinction between severe loss and heterozygous deletion.

       At the systems level, the retinal evidence favored impaired maintenance, reduced

resilience, delayed recovery, or stress vulnerability more strongly than increased sensory gain.

The best partial-dose retinal physiology identified in the project was reduced, not amplified,

electrophysiological response. Likewise, RTN4RL1 complete-loss neural models altered network
                                                                                                   253

construction but did not support a generic hyperexcitability narrative. MIR22 remained relevant

to epileptogenesis, but its strongest evidence came from complete loss after an epileptogenic

insult, not from heterozygous loss under photic stimulation.

3.5 Phase 5 deterministic integration and simulation

        Phase 5 formalized the earlier record into a layered evidence model and treated missing

downstream physiology as missing rather than as zero or as implied support. Its modeling and

simulation work did not create biological evidence; it examined methodological behavior. The

standardized simulation suite used 20,000 replicates per scenario/cell where applicable,

approximately 1.88 million primary stochastic draws/resamples. It showed that modest

heterozygous effects can be difficult to detect with too few independent biological replicates, that

pseudoreplication cannot substitute for independent clones or donors, that broad frequency scans

can generate false-positive signals without correction, and that limited data can make a

distributed mechanism appear spuriously single-gene.

        The integrated Phase 5 conclusion remained conservative: the deletion is best treated as a

candidate distributed visual-neural reserve lesion, not a proven single-gene photosensitivity

mutation. Broader retinal/RPE dysfunction, developmental/network modification, and stress-

dependent vulnerability are better supported than photic seizure causation.

5. Critical Assessment of the Work

5.1 What the project did well

           It froze a precise genomic reference and resisted syndrome-level overgeneralization from

larger 17p13.3 deletions containing different genes.
                                                                                                  255

       It enforced an evidence hierarchy: deletion -> dosage -> molecular perturbation -> spatial

opportunity -> cellular/developmental effect -> visual physiology -> phenotype -> causality.

       It distinguished genotype states rather than treating knockout, biallelic loss, pathogenic

variants, and heterozygous deletion as interchangeable.

       It preserved counterevidence and null findings, including compensation, normal

heterozygous states, reduced rather than increased response, and failure to identify magnocellular

or V1-specific signatures.

       It progressively narrowed the working hypothesis instead of escalating claim strength as

more literature accumulated.

       Phase 5 explicitly addressed reproducibility hazards such as low independent replicate

count, pseudoreplication, multiple-frequency testing, and model simplification.

5.2 Where the methodology remains vulnerable

       Literature dependence. Much of the mechanistic evidence is assembled across different

species, tissues, ages, perturbations, and assay types. Biological coherence can be real while still

failing to transfer quantitatively to this exact CNV.

       Sparse exact-genotype evidence. Most loci lack direct measurements in cells carrying the

complete heterozygous deletion. This is the largest limitation in converting mechanism into

CNV-specific biology.

       Potential ascertainment and confirmation bias. The project began from a visual-

neurological question, which creates a risk that visual or seizure-relevant literature was more

deeply sampled than unrelated phenotypic domains. The counterevidence framework reduces but

cannot fully remove that risk.
                                                                                                256

       Pathway and network inflation. Gene-dense CNVs can appear convergent because

broadly expressed genes participate in common cellular systems. Computational convergence

cannot substitute for direct combinatorial perturbation.

       Transcript and breakpoint uncertainty. The array-defined boundaries do not determine the

exact chromosome junction, transcript fate, nonsense-mediated decay, promoter/enhancer

effects, or allele-specific compensation for INPP5K and RTN4RL1.

       Expression-resource limitations. Single-cell and single-nucleus atlases have dropout,

donor, sampling, batch, regional-dissection, developmental-stage, and annotation constraints;

expression cannot establish dysfunction.

       Model dependence. Even disciplined ordinal or deterministic evidence models depend on

chosen weights, equivalence penalties, and missingness rules. Robustness analyses help identify

sensitivity but cannot convert sparse biology into calibrated causal probability.

5.3 Adversarial interpretation

       A skeptical reading can explain much of the current record without invoking

photosensitivity: the CNV may be biologically consequential because it affects several broadly

important cellular systems, while the apparent visual-neural convergence may reflect a mixture

of genuine retinal relevance, broad neuronal expression, and literature availability. That

null/weak-specificity alternative has not been eliminated. Conversely, the persistence of

retinal/RPE, trafficking, stress-response, and network-development signals across independent

evidence types argues against treating the interval as biologically irrelevant. The balanced

conclusion is therefore neither “causal” nor “nothing”: the deletion remains a credible candidate

reserve lesion whose disease-specific physiological consequences are unmeasured.
                                                                                                     257

6. Evidence Boundary and Falsifiability

         The highest supported causal level is: structurally defined heterozygous CNV with

selected demonstrated dosage consequences, mechanistically relevant retinal/neural pathways,

spatiotemporal opportunity, constituent-gene molecular/cellular phenotypes, and distributed

functional convergence. The project does not cross the required bridge into exact-CNV retinal

physiology, thalamic transfer, V1 flicker physiology, cortical inhibitory containment,

pathological frequency entrainment, long-range propagation, PPR, or visually triggered seizure

causation.

         Evidence that would materially weaken the functional-reserve model includes: normal

molecular and physiological behavior of an isogenic full-CNV heterozygous system across the

relevant retinal/neural assays under adequate power; complete compensation of predicted

dosage-sensitive nodes; absence of stress-dependent differences after calibrated challenge; or

demonstration that matched genomic control intervals show equal or stronger domain-specific

convergence under the same analytical pipeline. Evidence that would strengthen the model

requires exact-genotype perturbation and, ideally, rescue.

7. Path A - Continue In Silico Research with Mentorship and Peer Exposure

         This path treats the first-pass record as a reproducible research portfolio to be stress-

tested by domain experts rather than as a finished causal claim. The near-term objective is

methodological exposure: place the frozen pipeline, evidence tables, assumptions, and negative

findings in front of mentors or peers in human genetics, retinal biology, neurophysiology,

bioinformatics, or epilepsy research and invite attempts to reproduce, criticize, or break the

model.
                                                                                                 258

       Convert the Phase 1-5 evidence base into a clean public or mentor-reviewable repository

with frozen inputs, source manifest, scripts, environment specification, and explicit expected

outputs.

       Ask a mentor or peer to independently audit a bounded component first - for example the

CNV structural map, RPE convergence, genotype-equivalence coding, dLGN/V1 interpretation,

or Phase 5 statistical assumptions.

       Reanalyze one or two public datasets from raw or minimally processed data under

preregistered questions, rather than adding more narrative literature synthesis.

       Seek external review of the null model and the phenotype-ascertainment problem:

whether an unrelated gene-dense CNV would appear similarly “convergent” under the same

workflow.

       Present negative and contradictory findings alongside positive results. The project should

gain credibility by surviving criticism, not by maximizing the number of supportive mechanisms.

       Use mentor feedback to define a Version 2 research protocol. Do not alter this frozen

Version 1 record; any changes should be explicit amendments or a new project pass.

       A useful endpoint for Path A would be a mentor-reviewed methods note, reproducible

notebook/repository, poster, undergraduate research presentation, or small methods manuscript

that focuses on evidence integration and rare-CNV hypothesis discipline rather than claiming a

new clinical syndrome.

8. Path B - Transition Toward Wet-Lab Research

8.1 Brief research proposal

       Provisional title: Functional Validation of a Heterozygous 17p13.3 Microdeletion as a

Distributed Retinal-Neural Reserve Lesion.
                                                                                              259

       Central question: Does the exact heterozygous chr17:1,411,408-1,841,103 deletion

produce measurable, rescue-sensitive deficits in retinal/RPE maintenance, membrane/endosomal

biology, stress response, neural development, or visually driven network dynamics?

       Primary hypothesis: The CNV produces modest, distributed deficits in retinal/RPE and

neural functional reserve that may be compensated at baseline but become measurable under

controlled physiological or stress challenge. Photosensitivity is a downstream exploratory

phenotype, not the primary assumed outcome.

       Alternative/null hypotheses: (1) the heterozygous CNV is substantially compensated and

produces no reproducible phenotype under the tested conditions; (2) one locus dominates the

phenotype; (3) phenotypes are developmental/network-specific without retinal involvement; or

(4) retinal abnormalities occur without abnormal cortical flicker propagation.

8.2 Specific aims

       Create or obtain isogenic wild-type, heterozygous full-CNV, corrected/rescued, and

selected single-gene heterozygous human cellular lines. Confirm CNV structure and

transcript/protein dosage before phenotyping.

       Test retinal/RPE reserve using retinal organoids and/or RPE models. Priority endpoints:

phosphoinositide pools, lipid composition, outer-segment handling, lysosomal/endosomal

function, oxidative/DNA-damage response, dark-adaptation or electrophysiologic analogues, and

recovery after calibrated challenge.

       Test neural/network reserve in relevant human neuronal systems. Priority endpoints:

progenitor behavior, neurite morphology, synapse density, E/I-associated measures, calcium

dynamics, multielectrode-array firing/bursting, frequency entrainment, phase locking, and long-

range propagation.
                                                                                                  260

       Use genotype correction or targeted rescue to distinguish CNV-specific effects from

background variation and to identify whether observed phenotypes are distributed or locus-

dominant.

8.3 Minimal experimental design principles

       Use independent biological clones/donors; do not treat many cells from one clone as

independent replication.

       Predefine primary endpoints and flicker-frequency analyses; control family-wise or false-

discovery error across frequency scans and multiple phenotypes.

       Blind image/trace quantification where practicable and preserve raw data.

       Report null results and directionally contradictory effects.

       Power studies for modest heterozygous effects rather than knockout-scale effects.

       Treat rescue as a major causal discriminator.

8.4 Funding and fellowship strategy

       The most realistic transition is mentor- and institution-based. A trainee should first join or

collaborate with a laboratory that already has the required cell culture, retinal organoid/RPE,

electrophysiology, imaging, or network-analysis infrastructure. The project can then be adapted

to the host laboratory rather than attempting to acquire an entire platform de novo.

       NIH/NEI training mechanisms such as F31 or F32 are appropriate once the investigator is

in the corresponding predoctoral or postdoctoral training stage and working with an eligible

mentor/institution.

       NIH R15/AREA mechanisms can support small-scale biomedical projects at eligible

undergraduate-focused institutions, but the principal investigator must satisfy institutional and
                                                                                                261

appointment requirements; this is better viewed as a mentor/institutional route than an immediate

independent application.

       Vision-focused foundations can be relevant when the proposal is framed around retinal

cell biology or inherited retinal mechanisms; epilepsy foundations become more appropriate only

if the project directly tests seizure/network physiology rather than relying on indirect mechanistic

relevance.

       The grant pitch should emphasize the unusual strength of the preliminary in silico work

as a hypothesis-narrowing and assay-prioritization package, while explicitly stating that the

central physiological claims remain unvalidated. The strongest proposal is one that funds the

missing measurements, not one that presents the missing measurements as already solved.

9. Recommended Decision Point

       The two paths are complementary rather than mutually exclusive. The immediate low-

cost continuation is Path A: external methodological scrutiny, replication, and mentorship. Path

B becomes appropriate when a host laboratory, mentor, and assay platform can support exact-

genotype experiments. The first wet-lab study should be deliberately narrower than the full

project and should target one or two mechanistic bottlenecks with high discriminating value.

       A particularly efficient first experiment would compare isogenic wild-type, full-CNV

heterozygous, and corrected cells in a retinal/RPE model under baseline and calibrated stress. If

no reproducible molecular or physiological deficit is detected with adequate power, the

distributed reserve hypothesis should be weakened. If a deficit is detected and rescued, the

project gains a direct CNV-specific causal foothold before moving toward more complex

thalamic/cortical photic models.
                                                                                                262

10. Final Project Conclusion

       The first-pass in silico program establishes a structurally precise, biologically meaningful

17p13.3 heterozygous deletion containing multiple loci with credible dosage, retinal, membrane,

trafficking, stress-response, and neurodevelopmental relevance. Across the project, the evidence

repeatedly converged on reduced or altered functional reserve rather than on a single-gene or

simple hyperexcitable-retina mechanism. The strongest phenotype-level support concerns

retinal/RPE maintenance, photoreceptor integrity, intracellular material handling, stress

resilience, and neural-development/network organization. General epileptogenic modification

remains plausible but incompletely genotype-matched. Epileptic photosensitivity remains

downstream of an unmeasured physiological gap.

       Accordingly, the project does not conclude that the CNV causes photosensitivity, a

photoparoxysmal response, or visually triggered seizures. It concludes that the CNV is a credible

candidate distributed visual-neural reserve lesion whose most important unresolved questions

now require either independent methodological scrutiny or direct experimental perturbation. This

narrowing is the principal scientific accomplishment of the first pass.

11. Phase 6 Freeze Declaration

       PHASE 6 - COMPLETE

       The first-pass in silico Genome Research project is hereby frozen as Version 1.0 as of 30

September 2026. The controlling structural reference remains GRCh37/hg19 chr17:1,411,408-

1,841,103, nominally 429,696 bp, heterozygous. The frozen interpretive record is that the

deletion is biologically consequential and mechanistically consistent with a distributed visual-

neural functional-reserve model, while direct causation of epileptic photosensitivity by the exact

CNV remains unresolved.
                                                                                                   263

       Future research must not silently modify this record. New evidence should be handled by

one of three mechanisms: (1) an erratum correcting a demonstrable factual or computational

error; (2) a formally reopened analysis that states which frozen conclusion is being reconsidered

and why; or (3) a Version 2 research program that preserves Version 1 and documents all

changes in data, assumptions, methods, and interpretation.

       Null findings, contradictory evidence, failed hypotheses, and unresolved measurements

are part of the frozen scientific record and must remain preserved.

12. Current Forward-Looking Funding Notes (30 September 2026)

       These items are examples of funding structures relevant to future continuation and are not

claims of present applicant eligibility. Eligibility must be checked at the time of application.

       National Eye Institute: F31 predoctoral and F32 postdoctoral fellowship mechanisms

support mentored vision research training; F31 support can extend up to five years and F32

generally supports two to three years.

       NIH R15/AREA: designed for eligible undergraduate-focused institutions; current NIH

guidance states that the PD/PI must hold a primary appointment at the eligible organization and

that projects may run up to three years. Current NIH pages list total direct-cost limits up to

$375,000 for the applicable AREA mechanism.

       Foundation Fighting Blindness: the FY27 schedule lists an Individual Investigator

Research Award LOI deadline of 8 October 2026, a Clinical/Research Fellowship deadline of 15

October 2026, a Career Development Award deadline of 28 January 2027, and a Postdoctoral

Fellowship LOI deadline of 8 October 2026. These programs are oriented toward retinal disease

research and require program-specific eligibility and scope fit.
                                                                                               264

        CURE Epilepsy: Taking Flight supports early-career epilepsy investigators and requires

defined postdoctoral-level eligibility and mentor support. Any application from this project

should wait until the work contains a direct epilepsy/network physiology aim rather than using

retinal or molecular plausibility as a surrogate.
